Madame Beck (for it was Madame Beck—she had entered by a little door behind me, shod in the shoes of silence, so I had heard neither her entrance nor approach) had exhausted her command of insular speech with the phrase "You ayre Engliss," and now proceeded to work away volubly in her own tongue. I answered in mine. She partly understood me, but as I did not understand her at all—though we made together an awful clamor (anything like Madame's gift of utterance I had not hitherto heard or imagined)—we achieved little progress. She rang, ere long, for aid,

Delivered into the care of the *maîtresse*, I was ushered through a long, narrow corridor into a kitchen that was immaculate yet profoundly alien. It lacked a hearth or an oven; instead, a massive black furnace dominated one corner, a mechanical substitute for the domestic fires I knew. A strange sensation of relief washed over me when, rather than being abandoned to the culinary chaos, I was led to a small, enclosed room designated a "cabinet." A cook, dressed in a jacket, short petticoat, and wooden clogs, presented my supper: meat of an unidentified nature, served in a tart, acidic sauce that somehow tasted pleasant; chopped potatoes seasoned with a mystery blend I suspected to be vinegar and sugar; a slice of bread and butter; and a baked pear. Hunger conquered my apprehension, and I ate with gratitude.

Following the evening prayer, Madame Beck descended to inspect me once more. She commanded me to follow her upstairs. We traversed a series of peculiar dormitories—later learned to be former nun's cells, given the building's ancient lineage—and passed through an oratory. It was a long, low, gloomy chamber where a pale crucifix hung against the wall, flanked by two tapers burning with dim vigilance. She guided me to an apartment where three children slept in tiny beds. The air was stifling, heated by a stove, and thick with an overpowering scent that defied the circumstances: a pungent mixture of smoke and spirituous essence, unmistakably the smell of whisky.

Beside a table, where a candle guttered to nothingness, sat a coarse woman in a garish striped silk dress and a heavy apron, fast asleep. Completing the tableau, a bottle and an empty glass rested at her elbow. Madame Beck surveyed this scene with profound calm. Neither smile nor scowl marred her grave composure; no trace of anger, disgust, or surprise disturbed her equanimity. She did not even wake the woman. With serene gesture, she indicated a fourth bed as mine. Extinguishing the candle and replacing it with a night-lamp, she glided through an inner door, leaving it ajar to reveal her own chamber—a large, well-appointed apartment discernible through the opening.

That night, my devotions were entirely of thanksgiving. My journey had been strangely directed, my provisions unexpectedly secured. It was hard to believe less than forty-eight hours had passed since I left London, a passenger-bird with no guardian save the vague hope of the clouds.

I was a light sleeper. In the dead of night, I awoke to find the room hushed, yet occupied by a white figure: Madame in her night-dress. Moving without sound, she visited the three children, then approached me. Feigning sleep, I watched her study me for a long time. A curious pantomime ensued. She sat upon the edge of my bed for perhaps a quarter-hour, gazing at my face. Drawing near, she lifted my cap, turned back the border to expose my hair, and examined my hand resting on the covers. Turning to the chair at the foot of the bed where my clothes lay, she began to inspect them. Hearing her touch the fabric, I cautiously opened my eyes, driven by curiosity to see the extent of her scrutiny. It was thorough. Every article was examined. I divined her motive: to judge the wearer's station, means, and neatness through the garments. The intent was not malicious, but the method was intrusive. She turned my pocket inside out, counted the coins in my purse, and opened a small memorandum book, coolly perusing its contents until she extracted a small, plaited lock of Miss Marchmont's grey hair. She paid special attention to a bunch of three keys belonging to my trunk, desk, and work-box. Watching her withdraw to her own room, I saw them return moments later, bearing the impress of their wards in wax upon the toilet of the adjoining chamber. Once everything was restored with decent order, my property returned and clothes refolded, I waited for the verdict. What conclusions had she drawn? Were they favorable? Her face, now as stone-cold as the night, offered no response. The human warmth I had sensed in the salon was gone.

Having fulfilled her duty, she rose noiseless as a shadow and moved toward her chamber. At the door, she paused, fixing her gaze on the sleeping woman who snored loudly. Mrs. Sweeny—presumably Anglicized or Hibernian, perhaps Sweeney—met Madame Beck's eye with an immutable purpose. Madame's visitations for shortcomings might be slow, but they were certain. This was distinctly un-English; I was truly in a foreign land.

The following day revealed Mrs. Sweeny's origins. She had introduced herself as an English lady of reduced circumstances, a native of Middlesex speaking with a pure metropolitan accent. Madame Beck, relying on her infallible methods of verification, displayed singular intrepidity in hiring immediately, a trait proven in my own case. She accepted Mrs. Sweeny as nursery-governess for her three children. It was clear the lady was Irish, her station ambiguous. She boldly claimed to have raised the son and daughter of a marquis. I suspected she might have been a hanger-on, nurse, or washerwoman in an Irish family, her speech a smothered dialect overlaid with mincing Cockney inflections. She possessed a wardrobe of suspicious splendor: stiff, costly silks that fit her poorly, caps with real lace, and, the crown jewel of her inventory, a real Indian shawl—"un véritable cachemire," Madame said with unmixed reverence. Without this shawl, she likely would not have survived two days; by virtue of it alone, she maintained her position for a month.

However, upon learning I was to replace her, Mrs. Sweeny revealed her true nature, rising in her full power against Madame Beck. She descended upon me with concentrated weight. Madame bore this revelation with stoic endurance, so composed that I, ashamed of my own turmoil, forced myself to remain calm. When Madame briefly absented herself, a police agent appeared ten minutes later. Mrs. Sweeny and her effects were removed. Madame's brow remained unruffled; not a sharply accented word escaped her lips.

This brisk dismissal was settled before breakfast. Order was given, the policeman arrived, the mutineer expelled. The children's chamber was fumigated, cleansed, and aired; every trace of Mrs. Sweeny, including the fine essence and spiritual fragrance that marked her presence, was annihilated from Rue Fossette. All this transpired between Madame Beck's emergence from her chamber and her coolly pouring her first cup of coffee.

At noon, I was summoned to dress Madame. My role was a hybrid of governess and lady's-maid. Until then, she had haunted the house in her wrapping-gown, shawl, and silent slippers—a custom that would puzzle any English schoolmistress. Her hair, auburn and unmixed with grey despite her forty years, puzzled me. Seeing my embarrassment, she took the brush from my hand, arranging it herself without ungentleness. In other toiletries, she half-directed, half-aided me, displaying no temper or impatience. This was the first and last time I dressed her; henceforth, the portress Rosine assumed that duty.

Attired, Madame Beck appeared a figure of short, stout stature yet possessing a peculiar grace derived from proportion. Her complexion was fresh and sanguine, her eyes blue and serene. Her dark silk dress fitted her as only a French seamstress could achieve. She looked well, though somewhat bourgeois, and indeed she was. A strange harmony pervaded her person, yet her face offered contrast. Her features were stern; her forehead high but narrow, expressing capacity and some benevolence but no expansiveness. Her peaceful, watchful eye never knew the fire kindled in the heart or the softness flowing from it. Her mouth was hard, capable of a grimace, her lips thin. I felt she would be the right sort of Minos in petticoats.

In the long run, I discovered she was something else in petticoats as well. Her name was Modeste Maria Beck, née Kint; it ought to have been Ignacia. She was a charitable woman who did great good. There was never a mistress with milder rules. I was told she never remonstrated with the intolerable Mrs. Sweeny despite her drunkenness and disorder, yet Mrs. Sweeny departed the moment her removal became convenient. Similarly, masters and teachers were never faulted, yet they vanished and were replaced, none explaining how.

The establishment was both a pensionnat and externat. Day-pupils numbered over one hundred; boarders, about twenty. Madame possessed high administrative powers, ruling all these, plus four teachers, eight masters, six servants, and three children, while managing the pupils' parents and friends to perfection. She did so without apparent effort, bustle, fatigue, or fever. Always occupied, she was rarely idle. She had her own system for managing this machinery, a pretty system exemplified by turning my pocket inside out and reading my private memoranda. "Surveillance" and "espionage" were her watchwords.

Still, Madame knew honesty and liked it, provided it did not obstruct her will or interest. She respected "Angleterre"; regarding "les Anglaises," she preferred no women of other nations about her children if she could help it.

Often in the evening, after a day of plotting, spying, and receiving reports, she would come to my room, a trace of real weariness on her brow. She would sit and listen while the children recited their prayers in English—the Lord's Prayer and the hymn beginning "Gentle Jesus"—which these little Catholics were permitted to repeat at my knee. After putting them to bed, she would talk to me about England and Englishwomen, praising their superior intelligence and probity. She showed good sense and sound opinions. She seemed to know that keeping girls in distrustful restraint and blind ignorance was not the best way to raise honest women, yet she averred that ruinous consequences would ensue with continental children accustomed to restraint; relaxation, however guarded, would be fatally misunderstood. She was sick of the means she had to use but felt compelled to employ them. After discoursing with dignity, she would move away on her "souliers de silence," gliding ghost-like through the house, watching and spying everywhere, peering through keyholes and listening behind doors.

After all, Madame's system was not bad. Let me do her justice. Her arrangements for the physical well-being of her scholars were unsurpassed. Minds were not overtasked; lessons were distributed well and made incomparably easy. There was liberty of amusement and provision for exercise, keeping the girls healthy. Food was abundant and good; no pale or puny faces were seen in Rue Fossette. She never grudged a holiday, allowing ample time for sleeping, dressing, washing, and eating. Her method was easy, liberal, salutary, and rational. Many an austere English schoolmistress would do well to imitate her, and many would be glad to, if exacting English parents would permit it.

Ruling by espionage, Madame had her staff of spies. She knew the quality of her tools perfectly. While she would not scruple to handle the dirtiest for a dirty occasion—flinging them aside like refuse rind after squeezing an orange—I had seen her fastidious in seeking pure metal for clean uses. Once a bloodless, rustless instrument was found, she cherished it, keeping it in silk and cotton-wool. Yet, woe to anyone who relied on her beyond the point of her interest. Interest was the master-key of Madame's nature, the mainspring of her motives, the alpha and omega of her life. I have seen her feelings appealed to and smiled in half-pity, half-scorn at the appellants. None ever gained her ear through that channel, nor swayed her purpose. To attempt to touch her heart was the surest way to rouse her antipathy and make her a secret foe. It proved to her she had no heart to be touched; it reminded her where she was impotent and dead. Never was the distinction between charity and mercy better exemplified. Devoid of sympathy, she possessed sufficient rational benevolence, giving readily to people she had never seen, or rather to classes than individuals. "Pour les pauvres," she opened her purse freely; against the poor man, as a rule, she kept it closed. In philanthropic schemes for society at large, she took a cheerful part; no private sorrow touched her. No force or mass of suffering concentrated in one heart had power to pierce hers. Not the agony in Gethsemane, not the death on Calvary, could have wrung one tear from her eyes.

I say again, Madame was a very great and capable woman. That school offered her too limited a sphere; she ought to have swayed a nation, led a turbulent legislative assembly. Nobody could browbeat her, irritate her nerves, exhaust her patience, or overreach her astuteness. In her single person, she could comprise the duties of a first minister and a superintendent of police. Wise, firm, faithless; secret, crafty, passionless; watchful and inscrutable; acute and insensate—yet perfectly decorous—what more could be desired?

The sensible reader will not suppose I gained all this knowledge in one month or half-year. No! What I saw at first was the thriving outside of a large, flourishing educational establishment. Here was a great house full of healthy, lively girls, well-dressed and many handsome, gaining knowledge by a marvellously easy method without painful exertion or useless waste of spirits. They did not make rapid progress, but they were always employed and never oppressed. Here was a corps of teachers and masters more stringently tasked, doing the real head-labor to save the pupils, yet relieved in quick succession when work was severe. In short, this was a foreign school, its life, movement, and variety making it a complete and charming contrast to many English institutions.

Behind the house lay a large garden. In summer, the pupils almost lived outdoors among rose-bushes and fruit-trees. Under the vast, vine-draped *berceau*, Madame would take her seat on summer afternoons, summoning classes in turn to sit round her and sew and read. Masters came and went, delivering short

My days were suddenly filled with a purposeful rhythm, a blend of instructing others and sharpening my own intellect until there was scarcely a moment left for idleness. It was a refreshing change; I felt my faculties being polished rather than allowed to gather the dust of stagnation. The experience before me was vast. Villette is a cosmopolitan hub, and within Madame Beck's school gathered girls of nearly every European nation, spanning a wide spectrum of social rank. While the establishment was not republican in name, it practiced a rough equality in substance. At the desks, the young countess sat shoulder-to-shoulder with the young bourgeoise. Yet, appearances were deceptive. Often, the plebeian possessed a frank, courteous demeanor that the noblewoman lacked. The latter, however, held the upper hand in a delicate balance of insolence and deceit. There was frequently a dash of quick French blood mixed with the marshy phlegm of the north; the result was a glib, oily fluency in flattery and fiction—light, lively, and utterly heartless.

To give all parties their due, the native daughters of Labassecour possessed their own brand of hypocrisy, though it was coarse and transparent. When a lie served their needs, they delivered it with a careless breadth, untroubled by conscience. Not a single soul in Madame Beck's house, from the scullion to the director herself, felt shame in deception. To invent a falsehood was not a virtue, but it was the most venial of sins. "I have lied several times" was a standard item in every girl's monthly confession, heard by priests without shock and absolved without reluctance. Missing mass or reading a forbidden novel were different matters; these were crimes demanding rebuke and penance.

Though I was only half-aware of this moral landscape, I progressed well in my new sphere. After the initial perilous lessons, conducted on the edge of a moral volcano that sent sparks into my eyes, the eruptive spirit subsided. My mind was fixated on success; I could not bear the thought of being baffled by undisciplined disaffection in my first attempt at independence. Many nights I lay awake, devising plans to secure a hold on these mutineers, to bring this stiff-necked tribe under permanent influence. I saw clearly that aid from Madame was impossible. Her strategy was to maintain unbroken popularity with the pupils at any cost to justice or teacher comfort. For a teacher to seek her alliance during insubordination was tantamount to securing one's own expulsion. Madame took only what was pleasant and recommendatory, while rigidly requiring her lieutenants to handle every annoying crisis with promptitude, regardless of the unpopularity it might cause. Thus, I must rely solely on myself.

It was evident that this swinish multitude could not be driven by force. They required humor and patient endurance. A courteous, sedate manner impressed them; a rare flash of raillery did good. Severe or continuous mental application they rejected outright. Where an English girl of average capacity would quietly bind herself to a task of comprehension, a Labassecourienne would laugh in your face and reject it with, "God, how difficult! I do not want it. It bores me too much."

A skilled teacher would take this rejection instantly, smoothing every difficulty to the level of their understanding, returning the lesson modified, and laying on the lash of sarcasm with an unsparing hand. They would feel the sting, perhaps wince, but they bore no malice against this attack provided the sneer was hearty, not sour, and held up in a clear, bold type that exposed their incapacity, ignorance, and sloth. They might riot over three extra lines of a lesson, but I never knew them rebel against a wound to their self-respect; the little they had of that quality was trained to be crushed, and it preferred the pressure of a firm heel.

Gradually, as I gained fluency in their language and applied its nervous idioms to their case, the elder and more intelligent girls began to like me in their own way. Whenever a pupil was roused to feel the stirring of worthy emulation or the quickening of honest shame, she was won. If I could make their large ears burn under their thick glossy hair, all was comparatively well. Soon, bouquets began to appear on my desk in the mornings. In acknowledgment of this foreign attention, I sometimes walked with a select few during recreation. In conversation, I once unpremeditatedly attempted to rectify their distorted notions of principle, specifically expressing my views on the evil and baseness of a lie. In an unguarded moment, I stated that of two errors, I considered falsehood worse than an occasional lapse in church attendance. The poor girls, tutored to report in Catholic ears whatever the Protestant teacher said, reacted with an edifying consequence. Something—an unseen, indefinite, nameless barrier—stole between myself and these best pupils. The bouquets continued, but conversation became impracticable. As I paced the alleys or sat in the berceau, a girl never came to my right hand unless a teacher magically appeared at my left. Moreover, Madame's shoes of silence brought her continually to my back, as quick, noiseless, and unexpected as a wandering zephyr.

The opinion of my Catholic acquaintances regarding my spiritual prospects was naively expressed on one occasion. A pensionnaire, to whom I had rendered a small service, exclaimed one day as she sat beside me: "Mademoiselle, what a pity you are a Protestant!"

"Why, Isabelle?"

"Because, when you die—you will burn immediately in Hell."

"Do you believe it?"

"Certainly I believe it; everyone knows it, and moreover, the priest told me."

Isabelle was an odd, blunt little creature. She added, *sotto voce*: "To ensure your salvation up there, one might well burn you alive here below."

I laughed, as it was impossible not to.

Has the reader forgotten Miss Ginevra Fanshawe? If so, I must reintroduce that young lady as a thriving pupil of Madame Beck's. Upon her arrival in the Rue Fossette, a few days after my sudden settlement, she encountered me with very little surprise. She must have had good blood in her veins, for never was any duchess more perfectly, radically, unaffectedly nonchalant than she; a weak, transient amazement was all she knew of the sensation of wonder. Most of her other faculties seemed in the same flimsy condition: her likings and dislikings, her loves and hates, were mere cobwebs and gossamer. But she possessed one thing that seemed strong and durable: her selfishness.

She was not proud; and since I was *bonne d'enfants*, she forthwith made of me a sort of friend and confidant. She teased me with a thousand vapid complaints about school quarrels and household economy: the cookery was not to her taste; the people around her, teachers and pupils alike, she held to be despicable because they were foreigners. I bore with her abuse of the Friday salt fish and hard eggs, with her invective against the soup, bread, and coffee, with some patience for a time; but finally, wearied by iteration, I turned crusty and put her to rights—a thing I ought to have done in the beginning, for a salutary setting down always agreed with her.

I endured her demands on me in the way of work for much longer. Her wardrobe, regarding articles of external wear, was well and elegantly supplied; but there were other habiliments not so carefully provided. What she had needed frequent repair. She hated needle-drudgery herself and would bring her hose and other items to me in heaps to be mended. A compliance of some weeks threatening to result in an intolerable bore led me to distinctly tell her she must make up her mind to mend her own garments. She cried upon receiving this information and accused me of having ceased to be her friend, but I held by my decision and let the hysterics pass as they could.

Notwithstanding these foibles, and various others needless to mention—but by no means of a refined or elevating character—how pretty she was! How charming she looked when she came down on a sunny Sunday morning, well-dressed and well-humoured, robed in pale lilac silk, with her fair long curls reposing on her white shoulders. Sunday was a holiday she always passed with friends resident in town; and among these friends she speedily gave me to understand was one who would fain become something more. By glimpses and hints it was shown me, and by the general buoyancy of her look and manner it was ere long proved that ardent admiration—perhaps genuine love—was at her command. She called her suitor "Isidore"; this, however, she intimated was not his real name, but one by which it pleased her to baptize him—his own, she hinted, not being "very pretty." Once, when she had been bragging about the vehemence of "Isidore's" attachment, I asked if she loved him in return.

"As that," she said. "He is handsome, and he loves me to distraction, so that I am well amused. That is enough."

Finding that she carried the thing on longer than, from her very fickle tastes, I had anticipated, I one day took it upon me to make serious inquiries as to whether the gentleman was such as her parents, and especially her uncle—on whom, it appeared, she was dependent—would be likely to approve. She allowed that this was very doubtful, as she did not believe "Isidore" had much money.

"Do you encourage him?" I asked.

"Furious sometimes," she replied.

"Without being certain that you will be permitted to marry him?"

"Oh, how dowdyish you are! I don't want to be married. I am too young."

"But if he loves you as much as you say, and yet it comes to nothing in the end, he will be made miserable."

"Of course he will break his heart. I should be shocked and disappointed if he didn't."

"I wonder whether this M. Isidore is a fool?" I said.

"He is, about me; but he is wise in other things, *à ce qu'on dit*. Mrs. Cholmondeley considers him extremely clever; she says he will push his way by his talents; all I know is, that he does little more than sigh in my presence, and that I can wind him round my little finger."

Wishing to get a more definite idea of this love-stricken M. Isidore, whose position seemed to me of the least secure, I requested her to favor me with a personal description; but she could not describe. She had neither words nor the power of putting them together so as to make graphic phrases. She even seemed not properly to have noticed him; nothing of his looks, of the changes in his countenance, had touched her heart or dwelt in her memory—that he was "beau, mais plutôt bel homme que joli garçon," was all she could assert. My patience would often have failed, and my interest flagged, in listening to her, but for one thing. All the hints she dropped, all the details she gave, went unconsciously to prove, to my thinking, that M. Isidore's homage was offered with great delicacy and respect. I informed her very plainly that I believed him much too good for her and intimated with equal plainness my impression that she was but a vain coquette. She laughed, shook her curls from her eyes, and danced away as if I had paid her a compliment.

Miss Ginevra's school studies were little better than nominal; there were but three things she practiced in earnest: music, singing, and dancing; also embroidering the fine cambric handkerchiefs which she could not afford to buy ready-worked. Such mere trifles as lessons in history, geography, grammar, and arithmetic, she left undone or got others to do for her. Very much of her time was spent in visiting. Madame, aware that her stay at school was now limited to a certain period which would not be extended whether she made progress or not, allowed her great license in this particular. Mrs. Cholmondeley—her *chaperon*, a gay, fashionable lady—invited her whenever she had company at her own house and sometimes took her to evening parties at the houses of her acquaintances. Ginevra perfectly approved this mode of procedure; it had but one inconvenience: she was obliged to be well-dressed, and she had no money to buy a variety of dresses. All her thoughts turned on this difficulty; her whole soul was occupied with expedients for effecting its solution. It was wonderful to witness the activity of her otherwise indolent mind on this point, and to see the much-daring intrepidity to which she was spurred by a sense of necessity and the wish to shine.

She begged boldly of Mrs. Cholmondeley—boldly, I say: not with an air of reluctant shame, but in this strain: "My darling Mrs. C., I have nothing in the world fit to wear for your party next week; you *must* give me a book-muslin dress, and then a *ceinture bleu celeste*; *do*—there's an angel! will you?"

The "darling Mrs. C." yielded at first; but finding that applications increased as they were complied with, she was soon obliged, like all Miss Fanshawe's friends, to oppose resistance to encroachment. After a while I heard no more of Mrs. Cholmondeley's presents; but still, visiting went on, and the absolutely necessary dresses continued to be supplied, along with many little expensive *etcetera*—gloves, bouquets, even trinkets. These things, contrary to her custom and even nature—for she was not secretive—were most sedulously kept out of sight for a time. But one evening, when she was going to a large party for which particular care and elegance of costume were demanded, she could not resist coming to my chamber to show herself in all her splendour.

Beautiful she looked: so young, so fresh, and with a delicacy of skin and flexibility of shape altogether English, and not found in the list of continental female charms. Her dress was new, costly, and perfect. I saw at a glance that it lacked none of those finishing details which cost so much and give to the

Madame Beck was a creature of absolute consistency, a fortress of stoic calm that allowed no breach, let alone a deviation. Her forbearance extended to the entire world, yet her tenderness was reserved for no part of it. Even her own children, the very ones she was most solicitous about regarding their physical well-being and future prospects, could not draw her into the warmth of maternal affection. She watched them with vigilant, prudent eyes, but she never seemed to possess the impulse to gather them close, to press their rosy lips against her own, or to shower them with the soft, benignant caresses that define a loving mother.

I have often watched her sitting in the garden, observing the little bees as they marched in a distant alley alongside Trinette, their *bonne*. In her demeanor, one read only care and calculation. I knew she pondered anxiously what she termed "leur avenir"—their futures—but when the youngest, a puny yet engaging child, happened to spot her, broke from its nurse, and toddled eagerly down the path to clasp her knee, Madame Beck merely extended one hand to prevent the inevitable collision. "Prends garde, mon enfant!" she would say, unmoved. She would patiently allow the child to stand near her for a few moments, offering no smile, no kiss, no endearing syllable, before rising to lead it back to Trinette.

Her treatment of the eldest girl, Désirée, was equally characteristic, though in a darker vein. This was a vicious child, a poison to the household. "Quelle peste que cette Désirée! Quel poison que cet enfant là!" were the exclamations heard alike in the kitchen and the schoolroom. Among her many talents, she possessed an exquisite skill in provocation, sometimes driving her *bonne* and the servants nearly mad. She would sneak into their attics, ransack their drawers, wantonly tear their finest caps, and soil their best shawls. She would wait for her chance to raid the buffet of the dining room, smashing porcelain and glass, or storm the storeroom cupboard to plunder preserves and drink sweet wine, breaking jars and bottles solely to shift the blame onto the cook and the maid. When Madame Beck witnessed these acts, or received reports of them, her sole response was uttered with matchless serenity: "Désirée needs particularly special supervision."

Accordingly, she kept this promising olive branch constantly at her side. Never once, I believe, did she faithfully explain the nature of Désirée's faults, detail the evils of such habits, or show the consequences that must ensue. Surveillance, she believed, was the whole cure. It failed, of course. While Désirée was kept somewhat away from the servants, she turned her attention to teasing and pillaging her mother. Whatever belonged to Madame's work-table or toilet that she could reach, she stole and hid. Madame saw all this but pretended not to; she lacked the rectitude of soul to confront the child with her vices. When an item of value disappeared, requiring restitution, Madame would feign belief that Désirée had taken it in play and beg for its return. Désirée, not to be so easily cheated, had learned to use falsehood to aid theft, denying contact with any brooch, ring, or scissors. Carrying on this hollow system, the mother would calmly assume an air of belief, then ceaselessly watch and dog the child until she tracked her to some hiding place—a hole in the garden wall, a crack in the garret, or a cranny in the out-house. Once found, Madame would send Désirée out for a walk with her *bonne*, profiting from her absence to retrieve the stolen goods herself. Désirée proved herself the true daughter of her astute parent by never letting her countenance or manner betray the slightest sign of mortification upon discovering the loss.

The second child, Fifine, was said to resemble her dead father. Certainly, though the mother had given her a healthy frame, blue eyes, and ruddy cheeks, her moral being was not derived from her. It was an honest, gleeful little soul: a passionate, warm-tempered, bustling creature prone to blundering into perils. One day, she decided to tumble from the top to the bottom of a steep flight of stone steps. When Madame, hearing the noise (she heard every sound), emerged from the dining room and picked the child up, she quietly remarked, "Cet enfant a un os de cassé."

At first, we hoped this was not the case. But it was too true: one little plump arm hung powerless.

"Let Meess" (meaning me) "take her," said Madame; "et qu'on aille tout de suite chercher un fiacre."

In a *fiacre*, she promptly, yet with admirable coolness and self-possession, departed to fetch a surgeon. It appeared she did not find the family surgeon at home, but that mattered not; she sought until she found a substitute to her liking and brought him back. Meanwhile, I had cut the child's sleeve from her arm, undressed her, and put her to bed.

None of us, I suppose (by "we" I mean the *bonne*, the cook, the portress, and myself, all gathered now in the small, heated chamber), looked very scrutinizingly at the new doctor when he entered. I, at least, was occupied trying to soothe Fifine, whose cries (for she had good lungs) were appalling. These cries redoubled in intensity as the stranger approached her bed; when he lifted her, "Let alone!" she cried passionately in her broken English, "I will not you: I will Dr. Pillule!"

"And Dr. Pillule is my very good friend," was the answer, in perfect English; "but he is busy at a place three leagues off, and I am come in his stead. So now, when we get a little calmer, we must commence business; and we will soon have that unlucky little arm bandaged and in right order."

He called for a glass of *eau sucrée*, fed her with spoonfuls of the sweet liquid (Fifine was a frank gourmande; anyone could win her heart through her palate), promised more after the operation, and promptly began work. Assistance being needed, he demanded it of the cook, a robust, strong-armed woman; but she, the portress, and the nurse instantly fled. I did not like to touch that small, tortured limb, but thinking there was no alternative, my hand was already extended to do what was requisite. I was anticipated; Madame Beck had put out her own hand. Hers was steady while mine trembled.

"Ca vaudra mieux," said the doctor, turning from me to her.

He showed wisdom in his choice. Mine would have been feigned stoicism, forced fortitude. Hers was neither forced nor feigned.

"Merci, Madame; très bien, fort bien!" said the operator when finished. "Voilà un sang-froid bien opportun, et qui vaut mille élans de sensibilité déplacée."

He was pleased with her firmness; she with his compliment. It was likely, too, that his general appearance, his voice, mien, and manner worked in his favor. Indeed, when you looked well at him, and when a lamp was brought in—for it was evening and now waxing dusk—you saw that, unless Madame Beck had been less than woman, it could not well be otherwise. This young doctor (he *was* young) had no common aspect. His stature looked imposingly tall in that little chamber, amidst that group of Dutch-made women; his profile was clear, fine, and expressive. Perhaps his eye glanced from face to face rather too vividly, too quickly, and too often; but it had a most pleasant character, as did his mouth. His chin was full, cleft, Grecian, and perfect. As to his smile, one could not quickly decide on the descriptive epithet it merited; there was something in it that pleased, but something too that brought surging up into the mind all one's foibles and weak points—all that could lay one open to a laugh. Yet Fifine liked this doubtful smile and thought the owner genial; much as he had hurt her, she held out her hand to bid him a friendly good-night. He patted the little hand kindly, and then he and Madame went downstairs together; she talking in her highest tide of spirits and volubility, he listening with an air of good-natured amenity, dashed with that unconscious roguish archness I find difficult to describe.

I noticed that though he spoke French well, he spoke English better; he had, too, an English complexion, eyes, and form. I noticed more. As he passed me in leaving the room, turning his face in my direction one moment—not to address me, but to speak to Madame, yet so standing that I almost necessarily looked up at him—a recollection which had been struggling to form in my memory since the first moment I heard his voice started up perfected. This was the very gentleman to whom I had spoken at the bureau; who had helped me with the trunk; who had been my guide through the dark, wet park. Listening, as he passed down the long vestibule out into the street, I recognized his very tread: it was the same firm and equal stride I had followed under the dripping trees.

It was to be concluded that this young surgeon-physician's first visit to the Rue Fossette would be the last. The respectable Dr. Pillule being expected home the next day, there appeared no reason why his temporary substitute should again represent him; but the Fates had written their decree to the contrary.

Dr. Pillule had been summoned to see a rich old hypochondriac at the antique university town of Bouquin-Moisi, and upon prescribing change of air and travel as remedies, he was retained to accompany the timid patient on a tour of some weeks; it but remained, therefore, for the new doctor to continue his attendance at the Rue Fossette.

I often saw him when he came; for Madame would not trust the little invalid to Trinette, but required me to spend much of my time in the nursery. I think he was skilful. Fifine recovered rapidly under his care, yet even her convalescence did not hasten his dismissal. Destiny and Madame Beck seemed in league, and both had ruled that he should make deliberate acquaintance with the vestibule, the private staircase, and the upper chambers of the Rue Fossette.

No sooner did Fifine emerge from his hands than Désirée declared herself ill. That possessed child had a genius for simulation, and captivated by the attentions and indulgences of a sick-room, she came to the conclusion that an illness would perfectly accommodate her tastes, and took her bed accordingly. She acted well, and her mother still better; for while the whole case was transparent to Madame Beck as the day, she treated it with an astonishingly well-assured air of gravity and good faith.

What surprised me was that Dr. John (so the young Englishman had taught Fifine to call him, and we all took from her the habit of addressing him by this name, till it became an established custom, and he was known by no other in the Rue Fossette)—that Dr. John consented tacitly to adopt Madame's tactics and fall in with her maneuvers. He betrayed, indeed, a period of comic doubt, cast one or two rapid glances from the child to the mother, indulged in an interval of self-consultation, but finally resigned himself with good grace to play his part in the farce. Désirée ate like a raven, gambolled day and night in her bed, pitched tents with the sheets and blankets, lounged like a Turk amidst pillows and bolsters, diverted herself with throwing her shoes at her *bonne* and grimacing at her sisters—overflowing, in short, with unmerited health and evil spirits; only languishing when her mamma and the physician paid their diurnal visit. Madame Beck, I knew, was glad, at any price, to have her daughter in bed out of the way of mischief; but I wondered that Dr. John did not tire of the business.

Every day, on this mere pretext of a motive, he gave punctual attendance; Madame always received him with the same *empressement*, the same sunshine for himself, the same admirably counterfeited air of concern for her child. Dr. John wrote harmless prescriptions for the patient and viewed her mother with a shrewdly sparkling eye. Madame caught his rallying looks without resenting them—she had too much good sense for that. Supple as the young doctor seemed, one could not despise him—this pliant part was evidently not adopted in the design to curry favor with his employer: while he liked his office at the pensionnat and lingered strangely about the Rue Fossette, he was independent, almost careless in his carriage there; and yet, too, he was often thoughtful and preoccupied.

It was not perhaps my business to observe the mystery of his bearing, or search out its origin or aim; but, placed as I was, I could hardly help it. He laid himself open to my observation, according to my presence in the room, just that degree of notice and consequence a person of my exterior habitually expects: that is to say, about what is given to unobtrusive articles of furniture, chairs of ordinary joiner's work, and carpets of no striking pattern. Often, while waiting for Madame, he would muse, smile, watch, or listen like a man who thinks himself alone. I, meantime, was free to puzzle over his countenance and movements, and wonder what could be the meaning of that peculiar interest and attachment—all mixed up with doubt and strangeness, and inexplicably ruled by some presiding spell—which wedded him to this demi-convent, secluded in the built-up core of a capital. He, I believe, never remembered that I had eyes in my head, much less a brain behind them.

Nor would he ever have found this out, but that one day, while he sat in the sunshine and I was observing the coloring of his hair, whiskers, and complexion—the whole being of such a tone as a strong light brings out with somewhat perilous force (indeed I recollect I was driven to compare his beamy head in my thoughts to that of the "golden image" which Nebuchadnezzar

The summer heat was oppressive, a heavy blanket that seemed to press down on the very walls of the pensionnat. Georgette, Madame Beck's youngest daughter, had fallen violently ill with a fever. In a frantic display of precaution, Désirée and Fifine were whisked away to the countryside with Bonne-Maman, leaving the school vulnerable to contagion. The medical situation had become critical; Madame Beck, ignoring the return of her former physician, Dr. Pillule, summoned Dr. John, an Englishman, to take up residence within the school's division.

It was a bold move. Dr. John was soon attending to the proud Blanche de Melcy and the coquettish Angélique. While I observed a certain satisfaction in the doctor's demeanor at this mark of confidence, I knew that in a house of nuns and confessions, such a presence was dangerous. Rumors spread like wildfire through the gossiping schoolgirls and whispering kitchens, reaching the ears of parents who wrote letters of remonstrance. Madame Beck, however, was not a woman to be undone by a slip. Though a "little Jesuit" in her methods, she displayed a terrifying competence. She met the alarmed parents with a charming, easy grace, a "rondeur et franchise" that disarmed severity.

"Ce pauvre Docteur Jean!" she would chuckle, rubbing her soft white hands. "Such a dear young man! The best creature in the world!" She spun a tale of her own children's adoration for him, framing his employment as a temporary necessity for Blanche and Angélique's migraines. The parents, charmed by her maternal affectation, fell silent. The girls themselves joined in, loudly praising the physician, and Madame emerged from the crisis with flying colors, her reputation enhanced.

Yet, I could not fathom why she risked her standing so openly for Dr. John. The consensus of the house—pupils, staff, and servants alike—was that she intended to marry him. The disparity in their ages seemed irrelevant to them; it was simply to be. Appearances supported this theory. Madame, usually dressed in simple morning disarray, now appeared in full toilette: auburn braids, silk dresses, and laced brodequins, looking fresh as a flower for his early visits. Was she hoping to secure a comfortable future for him with her savings? Did Dr. John suspect her? He often left her presence with a mischievous smile, his eyes alight with a masculine vanity that suggested he encouraged hopes he never truly intended to fulfill. Or perhaps he did intend them. They were on excellent terms, and in a world where few marry for love, we waited to see what would happen.

What Dr. John waited for, I could not discern. His manner had changed; he was no longer merely attentive but expectant, vigilant, and absorbed. He seemed to range far beyond my understanding.

One morning, Georgette's fever worsened. She was peevish, crying out, "Minnie, Minnie, me very poorly!" I waited anxiously for the doctor, but when the bell rang and he was admitted, he did not ascend to the nursery as usual. Ten minutes passed, then twenty. The corridor remained empty. Where was he?

Driven by worry, I descended to investigate. The hallway was deserted. I listened intently. In the rooms nearby, pupils practiced instruments and sang barcarolles, their voices filling the air with "fraîchë" and "brisë." But in the small cabinet adjacent to the corridor—the portress's private room—I heard a different sound. A giddy, treble laugh mingled with a man's voice, soft and deep, uttering a desperate plea: "For God's sake!"

Then Dr. John emerged. His eyes were shining, but not with triumph; his fair English cheeks were flushed, and his brow bore the lines of baffled torture and anxiety. I stood behind the slightly ajar door, hidden in the shadows. He seemed mortified, his soul wounded by some cruel injustice. Who could hold such power over him? Madame was in her chamber; the room belonged to Rosine Matou, the portress—a pretty, fickle, and mercenary grisette. Surely, it was not her hand that had dealt this blow?

Before I could ponder further, Rosine's voice cut through the air, clear and sharp, singing a lightsome French tune. I glanced inside. She sat alone at a table, dressed in a smart pink jaconas gown, trimming a tiny blond cap. The room was bright with a July sunbeam, containing only goldfish in a globe and potted flowers.

A problem presented itself, but duty called. I ascended the stairs to inquire about Georgette's medicine.

In the nursery, the scene had shifted. Dr. John sat by the little patient's bed, having soothed her into a composed slumber. Madame Beck stood before him, discussing his health with exaggerated concern, noting his pallor and thinness. He listened with good-natured indifference, dismissing her worries as "trop bonne."

"What do you think, Miss Lucie?" Madame asked, turning to me. "Is he not paler and thinner?"

Rarely did I speak more than a monosyllable in his presence, content to remain the neutral figure he perceived me to be. Yet, this moment demanded a response. I chose my words carefully, making them significant.

"He looks ill at this moment," I replied, my voice steady. "But perhaps it is owing to some temporary cause: Dr. John may have been vexed or harassed."

I did not watch his face for confirmation, but I sensed the weight of my words. As Georgette asked for sugar water in broken English, Dr. John finally noticed that I spoke his tongue. He paused, as if considering a remark, but held his tongue.

When he finally rose to leave, bidding Madame good morning with a courteous yet dismissive air, the transformation in Madame was instantaneous. The animation vanished from her face, replaced by a stony, stern expression. She sighed—a deep, singular sound that seemed to drain the color from the room.

As the bell rang for morning school, she moved to a dressing table. In the mirror, she saw a single white streak among her nut-brown tresses. With a shudder, she plucked it out. In the harsh summer light, the texture of her youth had faded; the contours of her face had softened into something older. For the first time, I felt pity for Madame Beck. A calamity had struck her, and the hag of Disappointment greeted her with a grim "All-hail."

But what of Rosine? My confusion regarding the portress was profound. I passed her cabinet five times that day, seeking to understand the secret of her influence. She was pretty, young, and well-dressed—qualities sufficient to distract a young man like Dr. John. Still, I could not help wishing he were my brother, or that he had a sister to admonish him. I quickly discarded this thought as foolish; someone might as well preach to Madame about her physician.

Madame, I realized, was preaching to herself. She did not behave weakly or ridiculously. Lacking strong passions to overwhelm her, she relied on her genuine good sense and her demanding business to maintain her composure. She had matched an Apollyon of a predilection, fought a good fight, and, for now, overcome it. Brava, Madame Beck.

Beyond the walls of Madame Beck's establishment lay a garden, surprisingly spacious for the heart of a city. To my memory, it remains a place of gentle charm, though time, much like distance, possesses a softening power that transforms stone and pavement into something precious. Legend whispered that this site once housed a convent, a religious retreat swallowed by the expanding city centuries ago. It was said that a tragedy occurred there, leaving behind an inheritance of fear: a ghost story of a black-robed nun, a phantom seen wandering the vicinage on certain nights. Though the surrounding houses now choked the ancient grounds, relics of the past remained—ancient fruit trees, particularly a massive, dying pear-tree known as a "Methuselah." Beneath its gnarled roots, where moss clung to the earth, lay a glimpse of a smooth, black slab. The unconfirmed tale persisted that this was the portal to a vault, imprisoning the bones of a girl buried alive for a breach of her vows. Her shadow, it was said, haunted the moonlit thickets, her black veil mocking the timid eyes of those who dared to walk there.

Yet, stripped of such romantic rubbish, the garden possessed its own undeniable allure. On summer mornings, I rose early to claim it in solitude; on summer evenings, I lingered to keep tryst with the rising moon or to taste the cool kiss of the evening breeze. The turf was emerald green, the gravel paths stark white, and sun-bright nasturtiums clustered beautifully around the roots of the ancient orchard giants. There was a large *berceau* shaded by an acacia, and a smaller, more secluded bower nestled in vines climbing a high grey wall, where jasmine and ivy intertwined in a knot of beauty.

No doubt, at high noon, when Madame Beck's school was in full swing and the externes and pensionnaires exercised their lungs and limbs alongside the boys' college, the garden was merely a trite, trodden-down space. But at sunset, or during the hour of *salut*, when the externes had departed and the boarders were quiet at their studies, it was a paradise to stray down the peaceful alleys, listening to the sweet, exalted bells of St. Jean Baptiste.

One evening, I walked thus, detained longer than usual by the deepening calm, the mellow coolness, and the fragrant breathing of flowers answering the dew. I saw a light in the oratory window, indicating that the Catholic household was gathered for evening prayer—a rite from which I, as a Protestant, occasionally exempted myself.

"One moment longer," whispered solitude and the summer moon, "stay with us. All is truly quiet; for another quarter of an hour your presence will not be missed. The day's heat has tired you; enjoy these precious minutes."

The windowless backs of the houses bordering the garden, particularly one entire side, were skirted by the rear of the neighbouring college's boarding premises. This rear was mostly blank stone, save for attic loopholes for the servants and one casement in a lower story marking a master's study. Yet, despite this security, an alley running parallel to the high wall was forbidden to the pupils. Known as "l'allée défendue," any girl stepping there faced severe penalties under Madame Beck's strict rules. Teachers might enter with impunity, but the walk was narrow, flanked by neglected shrubs that wove a dense roof of branch and leaf, allowing sunlight to penetrate only in rare, dappled patches. After dusk, it was carefully shunned.

From the start, I was tempted to break this rule of avoidance. The seclusion and gloom of the path attracted me. For a long time, the fear of appearing singular held me back, but gradually, as people became accustomed to my habits and the peculiar shades of my nature—traits ingrained in me and inseparable from my identity—I became a frequent visitor. I tended the tintless flowers growing between the shrubs, cleared away the relics of past autumns, and cleaned a rustic seat at the far end. Madame Beck saw me working and smiled with apparent approval.

"Voyez-vous," she cried, "how clean this young lady Lucie is! You love this alley, do you not, mesdemoiselles?"

"Yes," I replied, "it is quiet and shady."

"C'est juste," she exclaimed with an air of kindness, recommending that I confine myself to it as much as I pleased, noting that since I was not charged with surveillance, I need not walk with the pupils. She added that I might allow her children to come there to speak English with me.

That night, I sat on the reclaimed seat, listening to the distant sounds of the city. In truth, they were not far; the school lay in the city's center, mere minutes from the park and palatial buildings. Wide streets nearby were brightly lit and teeming with life; carriages rolled through them bound for balls or the opera. The same hour that tolled curfew for our convent, extinguishing lamps and drawing curtains, rang out a summons to festal enjoyment for the gay city around us. I did not dwell on this contrast; my nature possessed few gay instincts. I had never seen a ball or an opera, and though I had heard them described and wished to see them, it was not a yearning to attain or taste, but merely a calm desire to observe a new thing.

A young crescent moon hung in the sky, visible through a space in the overhead boughs. She and the stars beside her were no strangers to me, for my childhood knew them well. I had seen that golden sign with the dark globe in its curve leaning back on azure beside an old thorn in Old England, just as it now leaned beside a stately spire in this continental capital.

Oh, my childhood! I had feelings. Passive as I lived, little as I spoke, and cold as I appeared, when I thought of past days, I could feel deeply. About the present, it was better to be stoical; about the future—such a future as mine—it was better to be dead. And in a state of catalepsy and dead trance, I studiously held the quick of my nature.

I remembered vividly whatever could excite me. Certain accidents of the weather were almost dreaded, for they woke the being I was always lulling, stirring up a craving cry I could not satisfy. One night, a thunderstorm broke; a hurricane shook us in our beds, and the Catholics rose in panic to pray to their saints. As for me, the tempest took hold with tyranny. I was roughly roused and obliged to live. I crept outside my casement, sat on its ledge with my feet on the roof of a lower building. It was wet, wild, and pitch-dark. Within the dormitory, they gathered round the night-lamp in consternation, praying aloud. I could not go in; the delight of staying with the wild hour, black and full of thunder, pealing out an ode language could never deliver, was too terribly glorious to resist.

I had longed, achingly, for something to fetch me out of that existence and lead me upwards and onwards. This longing, and all of a similar kind, had to be knocked on the head, which I did figuratively, after the manner of Jael to Sisera, driving a nail through their temples. Unlike Sisera, they did not die; they were but transiently stunned, turning on the nail with a rebellious wrench at intervals, causing the temples to bleed and the brain to thrill to its core.

That night, however, I was not so mutinous, nor so miserable. My Sisera lay quiet in the tent, slumbering. If his pain ached through his slumbers, something like an angel—the ideal—knelt near, dropping balm on the soothed temples, holding before the sealed eyes a magic glass of sweet, solemn visions, and shedding a reflex from her moonlight wings and robe over the transfixed sleeper, over the tent threshold, and over all the landscape without. Jael, the stern woman, sat apart, relenting somewhat over her captive, but more prone to dwell on the faithful expectation of Heber coming home. By these words, I meant that the cool peace and dewy sweetness of the night filled me with a mood of hope—not hope on any definite point, but a general sense of encouragement and heart-ease.

Should not such a mood, so sweet, so tranquil, so unwonted, have been the harbinger of good? Alas, no good came of it! Presently, the rude Real burst coarsely in—all evil grovelling and repellent as she too often is.

Amid the intense stillness of the stone pile overlooking the walk, the trees, and the high wall, I heard a sound. A casement creaked. Before I had time to look up and mark where, in which story, or by whom it was unclosed, a tree overhead shook as if struck by a missile, and some object dropped prone at my feet.

The clock of St. Jean Baptiste struck nine. Day was fading, though not dark; the crescent moon aided little, but the deep gilding of the point in heaven where the sun had beamed last, and the crystalline clearness of the wide space above, sustained the summer twilight. Even in my dark walk, by approaching an opening, I could have managed to read print of a small type. It was easy to see that the missile was a box, a small casket of white and coloured ivory. Its loose lid opened in my hand; violets lay within, smothering a closely folded bit of pink paper, a note superscribed, "Pour la robe grise." I wore indeed a dress of French grey.

Good. Was this a *billet-doux*? A thing I had heard of but hitherto had not had the honour of seeing or handling. Was it this sort of commodity I held between my finger and thumb?

Scarcely. I did not dream it for a moment. Suitor or admirer were thoughts my mind had not conceived. All the teachers dreamed of some lover; one, naturally of a credulous turn, believed in a future husband. All the pupils above fourteen knew of some prospective bridegroom; two or three were already affianced by their parents. But into the realm of feelings and hopes such prospects open, my speculations, far less my presumptions, had never once had warrant to intrude. If the other teachers went into town, took walks on the boulevards, or attended mass, they were very certain, according to the accounts brought back, to meet with some individual of the "opposite sex," whose rapt, earnest gaze assured them of their power to strike and attract. I cannot say that my experience tallied with theirs. I went to church and took walks, and am very well convinced that nobody minded me. There was not a girl or woman in the Rue Fossette who could not testify to having received an admiring beam from our young doctor's blue eyes at one time or other. I am obliged, however humbling it may sound, to except myself: as far as I was concerned, those blue eyes were guiltless, and calm as the sky, to whose tint theirs seemed akin.

So it came to pass that I heard the others talk, wondered often at their gaiety, security, and self-satisfaction, but did not trouble myself to look up and gaze along the path they seemed so certain of treading. This then was no *billet-doux*; and it was in settled conviction to the contrary that I quietly opened it. Thus it ran—I translate:—

"Angel of my dreams! A thousand, thousand thanks for the promise kept; scarcely did I venture to hope its fulfilment. I believed you, indeed, to be half in jest; and then you seemed to think the enterprise beset with such danger—the hour so untimely, the alley so strictly secluded—often, you said, haunted by that dragon, the English teacher—une véritable bégueule Britannique à ce que vous dites—espèce de monstre, brusque et rude comme un vieux caporal de grenadiers, et revêche comme une religieuse" (the reader will excuse my modesty in allowing this flattering sketch of my amiable self to retain the slight veil of the original tongue). "You are aware," went on this precious effusion, "that little Gustave, on account of his illness, has been removed to a master's chamber—that favoured chamber, whose lattice overlooks your prison-ground. There, I, the best uncle in the world, am admitted to visit him. How tremblingly I approached the window and glanced into your Eden—an Eden for me, though a desert for you!—how I feared to behold vacancy, or the dragon aforesaid! How my heart palpitated with delight when, through apertures in the envious boughs, I at once caught the gleam of your graceful straw-hat, and the waving of your grey dress—dress that I should recognise amongst a thousand. But why, my angel, will you not look up? Cruel, to deny me one ray of those adorable eyes!—how a single glance would have revived me! I write this in fiery haste; while the physician examines Gustave, I snatch an opportunity to enclose it in a small casket, together with a bouquet of flowers, the sweetest that blow—yet less sweet than thee, my Peri—my all-charming! ever thine-thou well knowest whom!"

"I wish I did know whom," was my comment; and the wish bore even closer reference to the person addressed in this choice document than to the writer thereof. Perhaps it was from the fiancé of one of the engaged pupils; and, in that case, there was no great harm done or intended—only a small irregularity. Several of the girls, the majority indeed, had brothers or cousins at the neighbouring college. But "la robe grise, le chapeau de paille," here surely was a clue—a very confusing one. The straw-hat was an ordinary garden head-screen, common to a score besides myself. The grey dress hardly gave more definite indication. Madame Beck herself ordinarily wore a grey dress just now; another teacher, and three of the pensionnaires, had had grey dresses purchased of the same shade and fabric as mine: it was a sort of everyday wear which happened at that time to be in vogue.

Meanwhile, as I ponder

I found myself compelled to smile—nay, to laugh—at Madame again, barely twenty-four hours after the little scene recounted in the previous chapter.

Villette possesses a climate as fickle, though perhaps less damp, as any English town. A night of high winds followed that soft sunset, and the subsequent day was a dry storm—dark, clouded, yet rainless. The streets were dimmed by sand and dust, whipped from the boulevards. I confess, even if the weather had been lovely, I would not have been tempted to spend my evening hours of study and recreation where I had yesterday. My alley, and indeed all the walks and shrubs in the garden, had acquired a new, albeit unpleasant, interest; their seclusion was now precarious, their calm insecure. That casement which had rained billets upon me had vulgarized the once dear nook it overlooked; elsewhere, the eyes of the flowers seemed to gain vision, and the knots in the tree-boles listened like secret ears. Some plants had indeed been trodden down by Dr. John in his search, and his hasty, heedless progress, which I wished to prop up, water, and revive; some footmarks he had left on the beds. But these, in spite of the strong wind, I found a moment's leisure to efface very early in the morning, ere common eyes could discover them. With a pensive sort of content, I sat down to my desk and my German, while the pupils settled to their evening lessons and the other teachers took up their needlework.

The scene of the "etude du soir" was always the refectory, a much smaller apartment than any of the three classes or schoolrooms; for here none, save the boarders, were ever admitted, and these numbered only a score. Two lamps hung from the ceiling over the two tables; these were lit at dusk, and their kindling was the signal for school-books being set aside, a grave demeanor assumed, general silence enforced, and then commenced "la lecture pieuse." This said "lecture pieuse" was, I soon found, mainly designed as a wholesome mortification of the Intellect, a useful humiliation of the Reason; and such a dose for Common Sense as she might digest at her leisure, and thrive on as she best could.

The book brought out (it was never changed, but when finished, recommenced) was a venerable volume, old as the hills—grey as the Hôtel de Ville.

I would have given two francs for the chance of getting that book once into my hands, turning over the sacred yellow leaves, ascertaining the title, and perusing with my own eyes the enormous figments which, as an unworthy heretic, it was only permitted me to drink in with my bewildered ears. This book contained legends of the saints. Good God! (I speak the words reverently) what legends they were. What gasconading rascals those saints must have been, if they first boasted these exploits or invented these miracles. These legends, however, were no more than monkish extravagances, over which one laughed inwardly; there were, besides, priestly matters, and the priestcraft of the book was far worse than its monkery. The ears burned on each side of my head as I listened, perforce, to tales of moral martyrdom inflicted by Rome; the dread boasts of confessors, who had wickedly abused their office, trampling to deep degradation high-born ladies, making of countesses and princesses the most tormented slaves under the sun. Stories like that of Conrad and Elizabeth of Hungary recurred again and again, with all its dreadful viciousness, sickening tyranny and black impiety: tales that were nightmares of oppression, privation, and agony.

I sat out this "lecture pieuse" for some nights as well as I could, and as quietly too; only once breaking off the points of my scissors by involuntarily sticking them somewhat deep in the worm-eaten board of the table before me. But, at last, it made me so burning hot, and my temples, and my heart, and my wrist throbbed so fast, and my sleep afterwards was so broken with excitement, that I could sit no longer. Prudence recommended henceforward a swift clearance of my person from the place, the moment that guilty old book was brought out. No Mause Headrigg ever felt a stronger call to take up her testimony against Sergeant Bothwell, than I—to speak my mind in this matter of the popish "lecture pieuse." However, I did manage somehow to curb and rein in; and though always, as soon as Rosine came to light the lamps, I shot from the room quickly, yet also I did it quietly; seizing that vantage moment given by the little bustle before the dead silence, and vanishing whilst the boarders put their books away.

When I vanished—it was into darkness; candles were not allowed to be carried about, and the teacher who forsook the refectory, had only the unlit hall, schoolroom, or bedroom, as a refuge. In winter I sought the long classes, and paced them fast to keep myself warm—fortunate if the moon shone, and if there were only stars, soon reconciled to their dim gleam, or even to the total eclipse of their absence. In summer it was never quite dark, and then I went up-stairs to my own quarter of the long dormitory, opened my own casement (that chamber was lit by five casements large as great doors), and leaning out, looked forth upon the city beyond the garden, and listened to band-music from the park or the palace-square, thinking meantime my own thoughts, living my own life, in my own still, shadow-world.

This evening, fugitive as usual before the Pope and his works, I mounted the staircase, approached the dormitory, and quietly opened the door, which was always kept carefully shut, and which, like every other door in this house, revolved noiselessly on well-oiled hinges. Before I *saw*, I *felt* that life was in the great room, usually void: not that there was either stir or breath, or rustle of sound, but Vacuum lacked, Solitude was not at home. All the white beds—the "lits d'ange," as they were poetically termed—lay visible at a glance; all were empty: no sleeper reposed therein. The sound of a drawer cautiously slid out struck my ear; stepping a little to one side, my vision took a free range, unimpeded by falling curtains. I now commanded my own bed and my own toilet, with a locked work-box upon it, and locked drawers underneath.

Very good. A dumpy, motherly little body, in decent shawl and the cleanest of possible nightcaps, stood before this toilet, hard at work apparently doing me the kindness of "tidying out" the "meuble." Open stood the lid of the work-box, open the top drawer; duly and impartially was each succeeding drawer opened in turn: not an article of their contents but was lifted and unfolded, not a paper but was glanced over, not a little box but was unlidded; and beautiful was the adroitness, exemplary the care with which the search was accomplished. Madame wrought at it like a true star, "unhasting yet unresting." I will not deny that it was with a secret glee I watched her. Had I been a gentleman I believe Madame would have found favour in my eyes, she was so handy, neat, thorough in all she did: some people's movements provoke the soul by their loose awkwardness, hers—satisfied by their trim compactness. I stood, in short, fascinated; but it was necessary to make an effort to break this spell, a retreat must be beaten. The searcher might have turned and caught me; there would have been nothing for it then but a scene, and she and I would have had to come all at once, with a sudden clash, to a thorough knowledge of each other: down would have gone conventionalities, away swept disguises, and *I* should have looked into her eyes, and she into mine—we should have known that we could work together no more, and parted in this life for ever.

Where was the use of tempting such a catastrophe? I was not angry, and had no wish in the world to leave her. I could hardly get another employer whose yoke would be so light and so easy of carriage; and truly I liked Madame for her capital sense, whatever I might think of her principles: as to her system, it did me no harm; she might work me with it to her heart's content: nothing would come of the operation. Loverless and inexpectant of love, I was as safe from spies in my heart-poverty, as the beggar from thieves in his destitution of purse. I turned, then, and fled; descending the stairs with progress as swift and soundless as that of the spider, which at the same instant ran down the bannister.

How I laughed when I reached the schoolroom. I knew now she had certainly seen Dr. John in the garden; I knew what her thoughts were. The spectacle of a suspicious nature so far misled by its own inventions, tickled me much. Yet as the laugh died, a kind of wrath smote me, and then bitterness followed: it was the rock struck, and Meribah's waters gushing out. I never had felt so strange and contradictory an inward tumult as I felt for an hour that evening: soreness and laughter, and fire, and grief, shared my heart between them. I cried hot tears: not because Madame mistrusted me—I did not care twopence for her mistrust—but for other reasons. Complicated, disquieting thoughts broke up the whole repose of my nature. However, that turmoil subsided: next day I was again Lucy Snowe.

On revisiting my drawers, I found them all securely locked; the closest subsequent examination could not discover change or apparent disturbance in the position of one object. My few dresses were folded as I had left them; a certain little bunch of white violets that had once been silently presented to me by a stranger (a stranger to me, for we had never exchanged words), and which I had dried and kept for its sweet perfume between the folds of my best dress, lay there unstirred; my black silk scarf, my lace chemisette and collars, were unrumpled. Had she creased one solitary article, I own I should have felt much greater difficulty in forgiving her; but finding all straight and orderly, I said, "Let bygones be bygones. I am unharmed: why should I bear malice?"

A thing there was which puzzled myself, and I sought in my brain a key to that riddle almost as sedulously as Madame had sought a guide to useful knowledge in my toilet drawers. How was it that Dr. John, if he had not been accessory to the dropping of that casket into the garden, should have known that it *was* dropped, and appeared so promptly on the spot to seek it? So strong was the wish to clear up this point that I began to entertain this daring suggestion: "Why may I not, in case I should ever have the opportunity, ask Dr. John himself to explain this coincidence?"

And so long as Dr. John was absent, I really believed I had courage to test him with such a question.

Little Georgette was now convalescent; and her physician accordingly made his visits very rare: indeed, he would have ceased them altogether, had not Madame insisted on his giving an occasional call till the child should be quite well.

She came into the nursery one evening just after I had listened to Georgette's lisped and broken prayer, and had put her to bed. Taking the little one's hand, she said, "Cette enfant a toujours un peu de fièvre." And presently afterwards, looking at me with a quicker glance than was habitual to her quiet eye, "Le Docteur John l'a-t-il vue dernièrement? Non, n'est-ce pas?"

Of course she knew this better than any other person in the house.
"Well," she continued, "I am going out, pour faire quelques courses en fiacre. I shall call on Dr. John, and send him to the child. I will that he sees her this evening; her cheeks are flushed, her pulse is quick; *you* will receive him—for my part, I shall be from home."

Now the child was well enough, only warm with the warmth of July; it was scarcely less needful to send for a priest to administer extreme unction than for a doctor to prescribe a dose; also Madame rarely made "courses," as she called them, in the evening: moreover, this was the first time she had chosen to absent herself on the occasion of a visit from Dr. John. The whole arrangement indicated some plan; this I saw, but without the least anxiety. "Ha! ha! Madame," laughed Light-heart the Beggar, "your crafty wits are on the wrong tack."

She departed, attired very smartly, in a shawl of price, and a certain *chapeau vert tendre*—hazardous, as to its tint, for any complexion less fresh than her own, but, to her, not unbecoming. I wondered what she intended: whether she really would send Dr. John or not; or whether indeed he would come: he might be engaged.

Madame had charged me not to let Georgette sleep till the doctor came; I had therefore sufficient occupation in telling her nursery tales and palavering the little language for her benefit. I affected Georgette; she was a sensitive and a loving child: to hold her in my lap, or carry her in my arms, was to me a treat. To-night she would have me lay my head on the pillow of her crib; she even put her little arms round my neck. Her clasp, and the nestling action with which she pressed her cheek to mine, made me almost cry with a tender pain. Feeling of no kind abounded in that house; this pure little drop from a pure little source was too sweet: it penetrated deep, and subdued the heart, and sent a gush to the eyes. Half an hour or an hour passed; Georgette murmured in her soft lisp that she was growing sleepy. "And you *shall* sleep," thought I, "malgré maman and médecin, if they are not here in ten minutes."

Hark!

As soon as Georgette recovered, Madame Beck dispatched her to the countryside. I felt a genuine pang of loss; the child had become a part of my household, and her departure left a void that made me feel poorer than before. Yet, I suppressed my complaint. I resided in a house teeming with robust life, and while I could have found companionship among the staff, I had chosen solitude. Each teacher, in turn, made advances toward intimacy, which I tested with varying degrees of caution. One was an honest woman, though narrow-minded, coarse in her feelings, and deeply egotistical. The second was a Parisian: externally refined, but internally corrupt, devoid of creed, principle, or affection. Beneath her polished crust of decorum lay a slough of moral decay. She possessed a voracious appetite for gifts, a trait shared by the third teacher, a woman otherwise characterless and insignificant. This third teacher was defined by avarice; in her, the love of money reigned supreme. The sight of a gold coin would bring a singular, green glisten to her eyes. Once, as a mark of favor, she took me upstairs to reveal a secret hoard—a mass of coarse, large coins totaling about fifteen guineas. She loved this accumulation with the infatuated dotage of a bird guarding its eggs, discussing them with a strange persistence despite her youth.

The Parisian, conversely, was prodigal and profligate in disposition. Her snake-like nature revealed itself only once, peering out cautiously like a reptile in the leaves of a bad novel. Had it emerged boldly, I might have surveyed it philosophically, but it merely rustled and recoiled at a demonstration of wrath, vanishing with a hiss. From that day, she hated me. She was perpetually in debt, anticipating her salary on dress, perfumes, cosmetics, and confectionery. She was a cold, callous epicure, thin-faced and sallow, with perfect teeth but lips like a thread and a frozen, craving eye. She mortally hated work, preferring what she termed pleasure—a heartless dissipation of time.

Madame Beck knew this woman's character perfectly. When I asked why she retained her, Madame answered plainly that it suited her interests, noting that Mademoiselle St. Pierre possessed a unique power of maintaining order. She exerted a petrifying influence, holding unruly scholars in check as a breezeless frost stills a brawling stream. While of little use in communicating knowledge, she was invaluable for surveillance. Madame admitted frankly that St. Pierre lacked principles or morals, but added philosophically that her demeanor in class was always suitable and dignified. "Neither the pupils nor the parents look further," she said, "and consequently, neither do I."

The school was a strange, frolicsome, noisy world where chains were hidden beneath flowers. A subtle essence of Romanism pervaded every arrangement; large sensual indulgences were permitted as a counterpoise to jealous spiritual restraint. Minds were being reared in slavery, yet every pretext for physical recreation was seized to prevent reflection on this fact. "Eat, drink, and live!" the Church seemed to say, promising robust bodies and feeble souls, guaranteeing final fate while leaving the cure of the soul to divine intervention.

In the ripe glow of summer, Madame Beck's house became as merry as a school could be. Doors and windows stood wide open, naturalizing sunshine in the atmosphere. We lived more in the garden than under a roof, classes held and meals partaken of in the "grand berceau." The air buzzed with holiday preparation, turning freedom almost into license. The autumnal vacation was two months distant, but a great day awaited: the fête of Madame Beck.

The conduct of the fête devolved chiefly on Mademoiselle St. Pierre, while Madame herself stood aloof, supposedly unconscious of the tribute being paid in her honor.
