She held my hand between hers, and at each favorable word gave it a little caressing stroke.

"In what other way is he good, Lucy?"

"Dr. Bretton is benevolent—humanely disposed towards all his race. Dr. Bretton would have benignity for the lowest savage or the worst criminal."

"I heard some gentlemen, some of Papa's friends, who were talking about him, say the same. They say many of the poor patients at the hospitals, who tremble before some pitiless and selfish surgeons, welcome him."

"They are right; I have witnessed as much. He once took me over a hospital; I saw how he was received: your father's friends are right."

The softest gratitude animated her eye as she lifted it for a moment. She had yet more to say, but seemed hesitating about time and place. Dusk was beginning to reign; the parlor fire already glowed with twilight ruddiness, yet I thought she wished the room dimmer, the hour later.

"How quiet and secluded we feel here!" I remarked, to reassure her.

"Do we? Yes; it is a still evening, and I shall not be called down to tea; Papa is dining out."

Still holding my hand, she played with my fingers unconsciously, dressing them now in her own rings, now circling them with a twine of her beautiful hair. She patted the palm against her hot cheek, and finally, having cleared a voice that was naturally liquid as a lark's, she said:

"You must think it rather strange that I should talk so much about Dr. Bretton, ask so many questions, take such an interest, but—"

"Not at all strange; perfectly natural; you like him."

"And if I did," she said with slight quickness, "is that a reason why I should talk? I suppose you think me weak, like my cousin Ginevra?"

"If I thought you one whit like Madame Ginevra, I would not sit here waiting for your communications. I would get up, walk at my ease about the room, and anticipate all you had to say with a round lecture. Go on."

"I mean to go on," she retorted, "what else do you suppose I mean to do?"

And she looked and spoke—the little Polly of Bretton, petulant and sensitive.

"If," she said emphatically, "if I liked Dr. John till I was fit to die for liking him, that alone could not license me to be otherwise than dumb—dumb as the grave—dumb as you, Lucy Snowe—you know it—and you know you would despise me if I failed in self-control, and whined about some rickety liking that was all on my side."

"It is true I little respect women or girls who are loquacious either in boasting the triumphs or bemoaning the mortifications of feelings. But as to you, Paulina, speak, for I earnestly wish to hear you. Tell me all it will give you pleasure or relief to tell: I ask no more."

"Do you care for me, Lucy?"

"Yes, I do, Paulina."

"And I love you. I had an odd content in being with you even when I was a little, troublesome, disobedient girl; it was charming to me then to lavish on you my naughtiness and whims. Now you are acceptable to me, and I like to talk with and trust you. So listen, Lucy."

And she settled herself, resting against my arm—resting gently, not with the fatiguing and selfish weight of honest Mistress Fanshawe.

"A few minutes since you asked whether we had not heard from Graham during our absence, and I said there were two letters for Papa on business; this was true, but I did not tell you all."

"You evaded?"

"I shuffled and equivocated, you know. However, I am going to speak the truth now; it is getting darker; one can talk at one's ease. Papa often lets me open the letter-bag and give him out the contents. One morning, about three weeks ago, you don't know how surprised I was to find, amongst a dozen letters for M. de Bassompierre, a note addressed to Miss de Bassompierre. I spied it at once, amidst all the rest; the handwriting was not strange; it attracted me directly. I was going to say, 'Papa, here is another letter from Dr. Bretton'; but the 'Miss' struck me mute. I actually never received a letter from a gentleman before. Ought I to have shown it to Papa, and let him open it and read it first? I could not for my life, Lucy. I know so well Papa's ideas about me: he forgets my age; he thinks I am a mere school-girl; he is not aware that other people see I am grown up as tall as I shall be; so, with a curious mixture of feelings, some of them self-reproachful, and some so fluttering and strong, I cannot describe them, I gave Papa his twelve letters—his herd of possessions—and kept back my one, my ewe-lamb. It lay in my lap during breakfast, looking up at me with an inexplicable meaning, making me feel myself a thing double-existent—a child to that dear Papa, but no more a child to myself. After breakfast I carried my letter upstairs, and having secured myself by turning the key in the door, I began to study the outside of my treasure: it was some minutes before I could get over the direction and penetrate the seal; one does not take a strong place of this kind by instant storm—one sits down awhile before it, as beleaguers say. Graham's hand is like himself, Lucy, and so is his seal—all clear, firm, and rounded—no slovenly splash of wax—a full, solid, steady drop—a distinct impress; no pointed turns harshly pricking the optic nerve, but a clean, mellow, pleasant manuscript, that soothes you as you read. It is like his face—just like the chiselling of his features: do you know his autograph?"

"I have seen it: go on."

"The seal was too beautiful to be broken, so I cut it round with my scissors. On the point of reading the letter at last, I once more drew back voluntarily; it was too soon yet to drink that draught—the sparkle in the cup was so beautiful—I would watch it yet a minute. Then I remembered all at once that I had not said my prayers that morning. Having heard Papa go down to breakfast a little earlier than usual, I had been afraid of keeping him waiting, and had hastened to join him as soon as dressed, thinking no harm to put off prayers till afterwards. Some people would say I ought to have served God first and then man; but I don't think heaven could be jealous of anything I might do for Papa. I believe I am superstitious. A voice seemed now to say that another feeling than filial affection was in question—to urge me to pray before I dared to read what I so longed to read—to deny myself yet a moment, and remember first a great duty. I have had these impulses ever since I can remember. I put the letter down and said my prayers, adding, at the end, a strong entreaty that whatever happened, I might not be tempted or led to cause Papa any sorrow, and might never, in caring for others, neglect him. The very thought of such a possibility so pierced my heart that it made me cry. But still, Lucy, I felt that in time Papa would have to be taught the truth, managed, and induced to hear reason.

May Day dawned with a promise of warmth, the air thick with the song of birds and a light mist that hinted at the sun to come. For the twenty boarders and four teachers, the morning began at five, a collective rising to prepare for a march led by M. Paul Emanuel. It was a fulfillment of a promise he had made to take us to breakfast in the country. I, who had initially been excluded from such plans, found my hesitation silenced by a sharp tug on my ear and a stern, imperial command from M. Emanuel himself. "Je vous conseille de vous faire prier," he warned, his tone carrying the weight of Napoleon. One such rebuke was sufficient; I resolved to join the procession.

The streets were quiet, the boulevards fresh as fields, and we moved along with a shared sense of joy. M. Paul possessed a unique ability to impart momentum to happiness, just as he could instill fear in moments of dread. He walked neither ahead nor behind but within the line, addressing everyone, though his conversation flowed most freely with his favorites. I, however, sought to remain slightly apart. Paired with Ginevra Fanshawe, I bore the weight of her arm, a burden of loveliness that I found increasingly difficult to carry as the day warmed. My strategy was to use her as a shield between myself and M. Paul, shifting positions constantly to avoid his gaze. My motivation was rooted in vanity; my new print dress was pink, a color that made me feel exposed under his scrutiny, much like a woman in a red-bordered shawl walking through a meadow where a bull grazed.

My efforts to evade him were futile. No matter how I shifted or adjusted my black silk scarf, M. Paul inevitably found himself beside Miss Fanshawe. Their relationship had never been smooth; his temper crisped at the sound of her English accent, and their dispositions clashed. He found her empty and affected; she viewed him as bearish and meddling. After changing his position six times with no success, he thrust his head forward, his eyes locking onto mine with impatience. "Qu'est-ce que c'est? Vous me jouez des tours?" he demanded.

Before I could explain, he grasped the root of my evasion. "A-h-h! c'est la robe rose!" he exclaimed, his voice dropping to an irate growl reminiscent of a lord of the meadow. "It is only cotton," I pleaded hurriedly, "cheaper and washes better than any other color." "Et Mademoiselle Lucy est coquette comme dix Parisiennes," he retorted, pointing to my hat, gloves, and boots. "Have you ever seen an Englishwoman like this? Look at her attire!" Though my companions wore similar items, none were smarter, perhaps even plainer, yet M. Paul had found his pretext. I braced myself for a lecture, but it passed as mildly as a summer storm's threat. With a single, bantering smile, he declared, "Courage! To tell the truth, I am not angry; perhaps I am even pleased that you have dressed so beautifully for my little festival."

"Ma robe n'est pas belle, Monsieur—elle n'est que propre," I insisted. "J'aime la propreté," he replied, dismissing my concern. The sun of his good humor triumphed that morning, consuming any clouds of irritation before they could darken his disposition.

We entered the countryside, amidst woods and lanes that would soon turn dusty but now glowed with May greenness and morning repose. We halted at a well surrounded by an orderly circle of lime-trees, ordered to sit on the grassy swell. M. Paul took his place in our midst, gathering those who liked him closest, while the fearful kept their distance. He began to tell a story, narrating with a diction that children loved and scholars emulated—simple in strength, strong in simplicity. His tale contained beautiful touches, sweet glimpses of feeling and hues of description that sank into my mind and never faded. He painted a twilight scene so vivid I felt I had never seen such a picture from an artist's pencil.

I lacked the impromptu faculty to create such beauty, which made me marvel more at his perfection. M. Emanuel was not a man of books, yet his mind was a library overflowing with wealth rarely found in print. To me, intellectually imperfect and weary of printed pages, his thoughts were collyrium to the spirit's eyes, clearing inward sight. I often wondered what delight it would be for someone who loved him as deeply as he loved himself to gather those handfuls of gold dust he so recklessly scattered to the winds.

After his story, he approached the knoll where I and Ginevra sat. "Were you interested?" he asked, demanding an opinion rather than waiting for it. "Yes," I replied in my usual undemonstrative fashion. "Was it good?" "Very good." "Yet I could not write that down," he said. "Why not, Monsieur?" "I hate the mechanical labour; I hate to stoop and sit still. I could dictate it, though, with pleasure, to an amanuensis who suited me. Would Mademoiselle Lucy write for me if I asked her?"

"Monsieur would be too quick; he would urge me, and be angry if my pen did not keep pace with his lips." "Try some day; let us see the monster I can make of myself under the circumstances. But just now, there is no question of dictation; I mean to make you useful in another office. Do you see yonder farm-house?" "Surrounded with trees? Yes." "There we are to breakfast; and while the good fermière makes the café au lait in a caldron, you and five others, whom I shall select, will spread with butter half a hundred rolls."

Forming a line, he marched us to the farm, which surrendered without resistance. Clean knives, fresh plates, and butter were provided, and we prepared a huge basket of rolls. Coffee, chocolate, cream, and new-laid eggs awaited us. M. Paul, always generous, would have ordered ham and preserves, but some of us, presuming on our influence, insisted it would be a reckless waste. He railed at us, calling us "des ménagères avares," but we managed the economy of the repast our own way.

He stood on the kitchen hearth with a pleasant countenance, happy to see others happy, surrounded by movement and abundance. We asked where he would sit; knowing he considered himself our slave and we his tyrants, we placed him in the farmer's great chair at the head of the long table. It was easy to like him, with all his passions and hurricanes, when he could be so benignant and docile. At worst, only his nerves were irritable, not his temper; soothe him, and he was a lamb who would not harm a fly.

Mindful of his religion, he made the youngest say a prayer before breakfast, crossing himself with the devoted simplicity of a woman. I had never seen him pray before, nor make that pious sign, yet he did so with child-like faith. I smiled pleasurably as I watched; his eyes met my smile, and he stretched out his kind hand. "Donnez-moi la main! I see we worship the same God, in the same spirit, though by different rites." Most of his brother professors were free-thinkers, infidels, or men whose lives lacked scrutiny, but M. Paul was like a knight of old, religious in his way and of spotless fame. Innocent childhood and beautiful youth were safe at his side. His vivid passions and keen feelings were kept in check by his pure honor and artless piety.

The meal was merry, led and controlled by M. Paul, whose social temper played unfettered among women and children. Afterward, the party was free to run and play in the meadows, though a few stayed to help the farmer's wife. M. Paul called me from among them to sit near him under a tree, where he could view the troop gamboling while he smoked a cigar. I sat at the tree-root, reading a pocket classic by Corneille—a book I disliked but he enjoyed, finding beauties I could not perceive. He listened with a sweetness of calm that impressed me, given the impetuosity of his nature. The deepest happiness filled his blue eye and smoothed his broad forehead. I, too, was happy—happy with the bright day, happier with his presence, happiest with his kindness.

By and by, he asked if I would not rather run to my companions. I said no, feeling content to be where he was. He asked if, as his sister, I would always be content to stay with a brother such as he. I believed I would, and I felt it. Then he inquired if I would be sorry if he left Villette and went far away. I dropped Corneille and made no reply. "Petite sœur," he said, "how long could you remember me if we were separated?" "That, Monsieur, I can never tell, because I do not know how long it will be before I shall cease to remember everything earthly." "If I were to go beyond seas for two—three—five years, should you welcome me on my return?" "Monsieur, how could I live in the interval?" "Pourtant j'ai été pour vous bien dur, bien exigeant."

I hid my face with the book, tears covering it. I asked why he spoke so, and he said he would not talk so again, cheering me with kindest encouragement. Yet, the gentleness with which he treated me throughout the day went to my heart; it was too tender, too mournful. I would rather he had been abrupt, whimsical, and irate as was his wont.

When hot noon arrived, the day glowing as June, the shepherd collected the sheep, and we began the soft journey home. A whole league lay between us and Villette, and the children were tired, their spirits flagging at the prospect of walking over flinty, glaring, dusty roads. This had been foreseen. Just beyond the farm boundary, we met two spacious vehicles hired for school parties. With good management, room was found for all, and in another hour, M. Paul safely consigned his charge at the Rue Fossette. It had been a pleasant day, perfect save for a breath of melancholy that had dimmed its sunshine for a moment.

That tarnish returned the same evening. Just about sunset, I saw M. Emanuel emerge from the front door accompanied by Madame Beck. They paced the center alley for nearly an hour, talking earnestly; he looked grave and restless, she amazed and dissuasive. I wondered what was discussed. When Madame Beck re-entered the house as darkness fell, leaving her kinsman Paul lingering in the garden, I thought to myself: "He called me 'petite sœur' this morning. If he were really my brother, how I should like to go to him just now and ask what presses on his mind. See how he leans against that tree, arms crossed, brow bent. He wants consolation; I know it. Madame does not console; she only remonstrates. What now?"

Starting from quiescence to action, M. Paul strode erect and quick down the garden. The carré doors were still open. I thought he was going to water the orange-trees, his occasional custom, but reaching the court, he took an abrupt turn toward the berceau and the first-classe glass door. There, in that classroom where I had been watching him, I found no courage to await his approach. He had turned so suddenly, strode so fast, looked so strange; the coward within me grew pale, shrank, and fled on the wings of panic.

I did not pause until I took sanctuary in the empty oratory. Listening with beating pulses and an undefined apprehension, I heard him pass through all the schoolrooms, clashing doors impatiently. I heard him invade the refectory, where the "lecture pieuse" was held under hallowed constraint. I heard him pronounce these words: "Où est Mademoiselle Lucie?"

Just as I summoned my courage to go down and do what I most wished in the world—to meet him—the wiry voice of St. Pierre replied glibly and falsely, "Elle est au lit." He passed, stamped with vexation, into the corridor. There, Madame Beck met, captured, chided, convoyed to the street-door, and finally dismissed him.

As the street-door closed, a sudden amazement at my own perverse proceeding struck like a blow. I felt from the first that it was me he wanted, me he was seeking. Had not I wanted him too? What had carried me away? What had rapt me beyond his reach? He had something to tell; he was going to tell me that something. My ear strained to hear it, and I had made the confidence impossible. Yearning to listen and console, thinking audience and solace beyond hope's reach, the moment opportunity suddenly and fully arrived, I evaded it as I would have evaded the leveled shaft of mortality.

Well, my insane inconsistency had its reward. Instead of the comfort and certain satisfaction I might have won—could I have put down the choking panic and stood firm for two minutes—here was dead blank, dark doubt, and drear suspense. I took my wages to my pillow and passed the night counting them.

On a Thursday afternoon, the air thick with the promise of rain, Madame Beck summoned me from my studies. "Lucie," she said, her voice cutting through the humid silence, "are you free to run a few errands for me in the city?"

I was at liberty, so I accepted her service with alacrity. She handed me a list of silks, wools, and embroidery threads required for the pupils' work. Clad in a coat suited for the gloomy, sultry weather, I stepped onto the threshold of the street door, only to be recalled instantly.

"Pardon, Miss Lucie!" Madame Beck cried, rushing back into the salon. "I have just remembered one more commission. Will your good nature not be overburdened?"

"By no means," I assured her, ready to oblige.

She returned with a small, exquisite basket filled with hothouse fruit—rosy, perfect, and tempting, nestled among dark green leaves and pale yellow exotic flowers. "Take this to Madame Walravens," she instructed, her tone shifting to something more intimate. "Leave it at her house, Numéro 3, Rue des Mages, in the old town. It is not heavy, and it will not mar your neat appearance. Tell her I send my felicitations on her fête. The walk is long, but you have the whole afternoon; do not hurry. If you are late for dinner, I will save a portion, or Goton will toss you a trifle. You shall not be forgotten, my dear."

"And one more thing!" she added, catching me as I turned. "Insist on seeing Madame Walravens herself. Give the basket into her own hands. She is a punctilious personage who values propriety above all. Adieu!"

Thus dispatched, I set out. The shopping for fabrics was tedious, a slow process of matching colors and textures, but eventually, the list was complete. Only the fruit and the message remained.

I welcomed the prospect of a long walk into the grim, ancient Basse-Ville. The sky above Villette was settling into a mass of black-blue metal, heating at the rim and inflaming slowly to a heavy, ominous red. I feared a high wind, for storms demand strength and action, things I yield to with pain. But the threat of rain, the sullen downpour, or the dark rush of deluge requires only resignation—a quiet abandonment of self to be drenched. In return, the rain sweeps the capital clean, transforming Villette into a phantom city of Tadmor. Let the floods descend; I must first deliver this basket.

I arrived at the address just as an unknown clock tolled the third quarter past five. The street was not a street but a square, quiet and overgrown with grass between broad grey flags. The houses were large and ancient, looming behind trees that hinted at gardens. Antiquity brooded here; business had long fled. Once, this quarter belonged to the rich and grand, centered around the church of the Magi, whose dark, half-ruinous turrets still overlooked the square. But wealth had flown, leaving behind only the potential for penury or empty, mouldering silence.

Crossing the deserted place, where raindrops the size of five-franc pieces darkened the pavement, I saw only one sign of life: an infirm old priest, bending and propped on a staff, the very type of decay. He emerged from the very house to which I was directed. As I paused before the door and rang the bell, he turned to look at me. He did not avert his gaze; perhaps he saw in my basket of summer fruit and my youthful lack of dignity an incongruous figure in such a scene.

When the door finally opened, it was not by a young *bonne*, but by a very old woman dressed in an antique peasant costume. Her cap was hideous yet costly, with long flaps of native lace; her petticoat and jacket were of coarse cloth, and her sabots looked like little boats. Her appearance was soothingly in character with the setting, though her expression was anything but. She was cantankerous, barely replying to my inquiry about Madame Walravens, ready to snatch the fruit from my hand.

However, the old priest, hobbling up, checked her. Addressing the aged woman in the aboriginal tongue of Labassecour, he persuaded her to let me enter. He then escorted me upstairs, ushering me into a salon that was large, with a fine old ceiling and church-like windows of colored glass. Yet, the room was desolate, cast in the shadow of the coming storm, looking strangely lowering.

Inside, a smaller room awaited, its single window blind drawn tight. Through the deep gloom, few details were visible, save for the outline of a picture on the wall. As I stared at it, bewildered, the image seemed to give way. It shook, sank, and rolled back into nothingness, revealing an arched passage leading to a mystic winding stair of cold, uncarpeted stone.

Down this donjon stair descended a *tap, tap* like a stick striking the steps. Soon, a shadow fell upon the stairs, followed by a substance that approached me. Was it actual matter? An obstruction partially darkening the arch?

It drew near, and I saw it clearly. I began to comprehend where I was. This old square was indeed the Quarter of the Magi; the three towers overlooking it were the godfathers of three mystic sages of a dead and dark art. Hoar enchantment prevailed here. A spell had opened an elf-land: the cell-like room, the vanishing picture, the arch, and the stone stair were all parts of a fairy tale. Standing distinct against this backdrop was the chief figure—Cunegonde, the sorceress; Malevola, the evil fairy.

She might have been three feet tall, yet she had no shape. Her skinny hands rested upon each other, pressing the gold knob of a wand-like ivory staff. Her face was large and set, positioned before her breast rather than upon her shoulders; she seemed to have no neck. There were a hundred years in her features, and more perhaps in her eyes—her malign, unfriendly eyes, framed by thick grey brows and livid lids. They viewed me with a sort of dull displeasure.

She wore a gown of brocade dyed a bright blue, full-tinted as the gentianella flower, covered with a large pattern of satin foliage. Over it, a costly shawl gorgeously bordered, so large for her that its many-colored fringe swept the floor. But her chief adornments were her jewels: long, clear earrings blazing with a lustre that could not be false; rings on her skeleton hands with thick gold hoops and stones of purple, green, and blood-red. Hunchbacked, dwarfish, and doting, she was adorned like a barbarian queen.

*"Que me voulez-vous?"* she asked hoarsely, with the voice of male rather than female old age. Indeed, a silver beard bristled her chin.

I delivered my basket and my message.

*"Is that all?"* she demanded.

*"It is all,"* I replied.

*"Truly, it was well worth while,"* she answered. *"Return to Madame Beck and tell her I can buy fruit when I want it, and as for her felicitations, I mock them!"* With that, the courteous dame turned her back.

Just as she turned, a peal of thunder broke, and a flash of lightning blazed broadly over the salon and boudoir. The tale of magic proceeded with the due accompaniment of the elements. The wanderer decoyed into the enchanted castle heard the spell-wakened tempest rising outside.

What was I to think of Madame Beck? She owned strange acquaintances; she offered messages and gifts at a unique shrine, and the bearing of the uncouth thing she worshipped seemed inauspicious. There went that sullen Sidonia, tottering and trembling like palsy incarnate, tapping her ivory staff on the mosaic parquet and muttering venomously as she vanished.

Down washed the rain; the welkin lowered deep. The clouds, ruddy a moment ago, had now turned deadly pale through their blackness, as if in terror. Despite my earlier boast about not fearing a shower, I hardly liked to go out under this waterspout. The lightning was fierce, the thunder crashed nearby; this storm had gathered immediately above Villette, bursting at the zenith. Forked, slant bolts pierced athwart vertical torrents; red zigzags interlaced a descent blanched as white metal, all breaking from a sky heavily black in its swollen abundance.

Leaving Madame Walravens' inhospitable salon, I retreated to her cold staircase. There was a seat on the landing, and I waited. Someone glided along the gallery above; it was the old priest.

*"Indeed, Mademoiselle shall not sit there,"* he said. *"It would displease our benefactor if he knew a stranger was so treated in this house."*

He begged me so earnestly to return to the salon that, without discourtesy, I could not refuse. The smaller room was better furnished and more habitable. Thither he introduced me. Partially withdrawing the blind, he disclosed what seemed more like an oratory than a boudoir—a very solemn little chamber dedicated to relics and remembrance rather than present comfort.

The good father sat down as if to keep me company, but instead of conversing, he took out a book, fastened his eyes on the page, and employed his lips in whispering what sounded like a prayer or litany. A yellow electric light from the sky gilded his bald head; his figure remained in deep, purple shade. He sat still as sculpture, seemingly forgetting me for his prayers, looking up only when a fiercer bolt or harsher rattle told of nearing danger. Even then, it was not in fear, but in seeming awe, that he raised his eyes. I too was awe-struck. Being, however, under no pressure of slavish terror, my thoughts and observations remained free.

Truth be told, I began to fancy that the old priest resembled Père Silas, before whom I had knelt in the church of the Béguinage. The idea was vague, for I had seen my confessor only in dusk and profile, yet I seemed to trace a likeness. I thought also I recognized the voice. While I watched him, he betrayed, by one lifted look, that he felt my scrutiny. I turned to note the room; it too held a half-mystic interest.

Beside a cross of curiously carved old ivory, yellow with time and sloped above a dark-red *prie-dieu* furnished with a rich missal and ebon rosary, hung the picture whose dim outline had drawn my eyes before—the picture that moved, fell away with the wall, and let in phantoms. Imperfectly seen, I had taken it for a Madonna; revealed by clearer light, it proved to be a woman's portrait in a nun's dress. The face, though not beautiful, was pleasing: pale, young, and shaded with the dejection of grief or ill health. It was not intellectual; its amiability was that of a weak frame, inactive passions, acquiescent habits. Yet I looked long at that picture and could not choose but stare.

The old priest, who at first had seemed so deaf and infirm, must have retained his faculties in tolerable preservation. Absorbed in his book, without once lifting his head, he perceived the point towards which my attention was drawn. In a slow, distinct voice, he dropped these four observations concerning it:

"By that aged lady, Madame Walravens?" I inquired, fancying that I had discovered in the incurable grief of bereavement a key to that same aged lady's desperate ill-humor.

The father shook his head with a half-smile.

"No, no," he said. "A grand-dame's affection for her children's children may be great, and her sorrow for their loss lively; but it is only the affianced lover, to whom Fate, Faith, and Death have trebly denied the bliss of union, who mourns what he has lost, as Justine Marie is still mourned."

I thought the father rather wished to be questioned, so I inquired who had lost and who still mourned "Justine Marie." I received, in reply, a little romantic narrative told not unimpressively, accompanied by the now subsiding storm. I must say it might have been made much more truly impressive if there had been less French, Rousseau-like sentimentalizing and wire-drawing, and rather more healthful carelessness of effect. But the worthy father was obviously a Frenchman born and bred—a true son of Rome. When he did lift his eyes, he looked at me out of their corners with more and sharper subtlety than one would have thought could survive the wear and tear of seventy years. Yet, I believe, he was a good old man.

The hero of his tale was a former pupil of his, whom he now called his benefactor. This man had loved Justine Marie, the daughter of rich parents, at a time when his own worldly prospects justified his aspiring to a well-dowered hand. The pupil's father, once a rich banker, had failed, died, and left behind only debts and destitution. The son was then forbidden to think of Marie, especially since that old witch of a grand-dame, Madame Walravens, opposed the match with all the violence of a temper which deformity made sometimes demoniac. The mild Marie had neither the treachery to be false nor the force to be quite staunch to her lover; she gave up her first suitor but, refusing to accept a second with a heavier purse, withdrew to a convent and died in her noviciate.

Lasting anguish had taken possession of the faithful heart which worshipped her, and the truth of that love and grief had been shown in a manner which touched even me as I listened.

Some years after Justine Marie's death, ruin had come on her house too. Her father, by nominal calling a jeweller but

"Oubliez les Professeurs," Madame Beck advised, her voice carrying the weight of a seasoned educator. She was a wise woman, yet in that moment, her wisdom faltered. That night, she should have left me in a state of calm indifference, isolated in my own quiet estimation, untethered from the second person I was destined to forget. Instead, she had ignited a fire I could not extinguish.

How could they expect me to forget him? The "wiseheads" had devised a cunning trap. They unveiled his goodness, transforming my dear little man into a stainless hero. They prattled endlessly about his capacity for love, leaving me with no prior evidence to contradict their narrative. Before this day, I had only known him as jealous and suspicious, marked by tendernesses that arrived like warm air and vanished like early dew, dried instantly by the heat of his irritabilities. But Père Silas and Madame Beck, working in concert, had opened the sacred chamber of his heart. They revealed a grand, singular love—a child of southern youth, born strong and perfect, laughing at death itself, despising the corruption of matter, and clinging to the immortal spirit. They showed me a fidelity proven by the consecration of his energies to an unselfish purpose, attested by limitless sacrifice. For those once dear to her, he had laid down vengeance and taken up a cross.

As for Justine Marie, I knew her well enough. She was one of Madame Beck's phlegmatics: pale, slow, inert, yet kind-natured and neutral. If she wore angels' wings, it was merely the poet-fancy of others; if her forehead shone with a halo, it was the reflection of someone else's fire. Was I to be frightened by her? Was the image of a pale, dead nun to stand as an eternal barrier between me and the man I loved? What of the charities that consumed his worldly goods? What of his heart sworn to virginity?

Madame Beck and Père Silas, you should not have suggested these questions. They became the deepest puzzle, the strongest obstruction, and the keenest stimulus I had ever known. For a week of nights and days, I fell asleep only to wake upon these two haunting inquiries. In the entire world, there was no answer to them except where a dark little man stood, sat, or lectured beneath the brim of a *bonnet grec* and within the frayed girth of a soot-stained paletot.

After my visit to the Rue des Mages, I desperately wanted to see him again. Knowing what I now knew, I felt his countenance would offer a page more lucid, more interesting than ever. I longed to trace in his features the imprint of that primitive devotion, the signs of that half-knightly, half-saintly chivalry the priest had imputed to his nature. He had become my Christian hero, and I wanted to view him through that lens.

Opportunity did not wait. My new impressions underwent their test the very next day. Yes, I was granted an interview with my "Christian hero"—an encounter neither heroic nor biblical, but lively in its own chaotic way.

About three in the afternoon, the peace of the first *classe*, safely established under the serene sway of Madame Beck, who was personally delivering one of her orderly lessons, suffered a sudden fracture. A wild *paletot* burst in.

I was the quietest soul in the room, eased of responsibility by Madame Beck's presence, soothed by her uniform tones, and edified by her clear exposition. I sat bent over my desk, drawing—copying an elaborate line engraving with tedious precision, for that was my practical notion of art. Strangely, I took great pleasure in the labor, producing curiously finical Chinese facsimiles of steel plates, things I valued highly in those days.

Then, disaster struck. My drawing, my pencils, my precious copy were gathered into one crushed handful and perished from my sight. I appeared to be shaken out of my chair like a withered nutmeg emptied from a spice box. My chair and desk were seized by the wild *paletot*, one under each sleeve, and borne away. In a second, I followed the furniture; in two minutes, we were fixed in the center of the *grand salle*—a vast adjoining room seldom used save for dancing and choral singing—fixed with an emphasis that seemed to prohibit any hope of movement.

Having partially collected my scattered wits, I found myself in the presence of two men, gentlemen, I suppose. One was dark, the other light; one possessed a stiff, half-military air in a braided *surtout*, while the other bore the careless aspect of a student or artist. Both flourished in the full magnificence of moustaches, whiskers, and imperial. M. Emanuel stood apart, his countenance and eyes expressing strong choler, holding forth his hand in a tribune gesture.

"Mademoiselle," he said, "your business is to prove to these gentlemen that I am no liar. You will answer, to the best of your ability, such questions as they shall put. You will also write on such a theme as they shall select. In their eyes, it appears, I hold the position of an unprincipled impostor. I write essays and, with deliberate forgery, sign my pupils' names, boasting of them as my work. You will disprove this charge."

*Grand ciel!* Here was the show-trial, so long evaded, coming upon me like a thunderclap. These two fine, braided, mustachioed, sneering personages were none other than the dandy professors of the college—Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte—a pair of cold-blooded fops and pedants, skeptics, and scoffers. It seemed M. Paul had rashly exhibited something I had written—something he had never praised or even mentioned in my hearing, and which I deemed forgotten. The essay was not remarkable; it only *seemed* so compared to the average productions of foreign schoolgirls. In an English establishment, it would have passed unnoticed. Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte had deemed it necessary to question its genuineness and insinuate a cheat. Now, I was to bear witness to the truth and endure the torture of their examination.

A memorable scene ensued.

They began with classics. A dead blank. They moved on to French history. I hardly knew Mérovée from Pharamond. They tried me in various sciences, receiving only a shake of the head and an unchanging "Je n'en sais rien."

After an expressive pause, they proceeded to matters of general information, broaching subjects I knew well and had often reflected upon. M. Emanuel, who had hitherto stood looking on, dark as the winter solstice, brightened somewhat; he thought I would now show myself no fool.

He learned his error. Though answers surged up fast, my mind filling like a rising well, ideas were there but not words. I either could not, or would not, speak—I am not sure which. Partly, my nerves were wrong; partly, my humor was crossed.

I heard one of my examiners, the one in the braided *surtout*, whisper to his co-professor, "Est-elle donc idiote?"

"Yes," I thought, "an idiot she is, and always will be, for such as you."

But I suffered—cruelly. I saw the damp gather on M. Paul's brow, and his eye spoke a passionate yet sad reproach. He would not believe in my total lack of popular cleverness; he thought I *could* be prompt if I *would*.

Finally, to relieve him, the professors, and myself, I stammered out: "Gentlemen, you had better let me go; you will get no good of me; as you say, I am an idiot."

I wish I could have spoken with calm and dignity, or that my sense had sufficed to make me hold my tongue. That traitor tongue tripped and faltered. Beholding the judges cast a hard look of triumph on M. Emanuel and hearing the distressed tremor of my own voice, I burst out in a fit of choking tears. The emotion was far more anger than grief; had I been a man and strong, I could have challenged that pair on the spot—but it was emotion, and I would rather have been scourged than betray it.

The incapables! Could they not see the crude hand of a novice in that composition they called a forgery? The subject was classical. When M. Paul dictated the theme, I heard it for the first time; the matter was new to me, and I had no material for its treatment. But I had gone to the library, read up the facts, laboriously constructed a skeleton from the dry bones of the real, and then clothed them, trying to breathe life into them. With me, it was a difficult and anxious time until my facts were found, selected, and properly jointed. I could not rest from research until I was satisfied of correct anatomy. The strength of my inward repugnance to the idea of flaw or falsity sometimes enabled me to shun egregious blunders, but the knowledge was not there in my head, ready and mellow. It had not been sown in spring, grown in summer, harvested in autumn, and garnered through winter. Whatever I wanted, I had to go out and gather fresh; glean wild herbs into my lap and shred them green into the pot. Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte did not perceive this. They mistook my work for that of a ripe scholar.

They would not let me go. I must sit down and write before them. As I dipped my pen in the ink with a shaking hand and surveyed the white paper with eyes half-blinded and overflowing, one of my judges began to mince an apology for the pain he caused.

"Nous agissons dans l'intérêt de la vérité. Nous ne voulons pas vous blesser," he said.

Scorn gave me nerve. I answered only, "Dictate, Monsieur."

Rochemorte named the theme: "Human Justice."

*Human Justice!* What was I to make of it? A blank, cold abstraction, unsuggestive of any inspiring idea. There stood M. Emanuel, sad as Saul and stern as Joab, while his accusers triumphed.

At these two, I looked. I was gathering my courage to tell them I would neither write nor speak another word for their satisfaction, that their theme did not suit, nor their presence inspire me, and that whoever threw the shadow of a doubt on M. Emanuel's honor outraged the truth they claimed to champion. I *meant* to utter all this when suddenly, a light darted across my memory.

Those two faces looking out from the forest of long hair, moustache, and whisker—those two cold yet bold, trustless yet presumptuous visages—were the very same that, projected in full gaslight from behind the pillars of a portico, had half frightened me to death on the night of my desolate arrival in Villette. These, I felt morally certain, were the very heroes who had driven a friendless foreigner beyond her reckoning and her strength, chasing her breathless over a whole quarter of the town.

"Pious mentors!" I thought. "Pure guides for youth! If 'Human Justice' were what she ought to be, you two would scarce hold your present post, or enjoy your present credit."

An idea seized me, and I fell to work. "Human Justice" rushed before me in a novel guise: a red, random beldame with arms akimbo. I saw her in her house, the den of confusion: servants called to her for orders she did not give; beggars stood at her door starving unnoticed; a swarm of sick and quarrelsome children crawled round her feet, yelling appeals for notice and redress. The honest woman cared for none of these things. She had a warm seat by the fire, her own solace in a short black pipe and a bottle of Mrs. Sweeny's soothing syrup. She smoked and sipped, enjoying her paradise. Whenever a cry of the suffering souls pierced her ears too keenly, my jolly dame seized the poker or the hearth-brush. If the offender was weak, wronged, and sickly, she effectively settled him. If he was strong, lively, and violent, she only menaced, then plunged her hand in her deep pouch and flung a liberal shower of sugar-plums.

Such was the sketch of "Human Justice," scratched hurriedly on paper and placed at the service of Messrs. Boissec and Rochemorte. M. Emanuel read it over my shoulder. Waiting no comment, I curtsied to the trio and withdrew.

After school that day, M. Paul and I met again. Of course, the meeting did not run smoothly at first; there was a crow to pluck with him. That forced examination could not be immediately digested. A crabbed dialogue terminated in my being called "une petite moqueuse et sans-cœur," and in Monsieur's temporary departure.

Not wishing him to go quite away, only desiring he should feel that such a transport as he had given way to could not be indulged with perfect impunity, I was not sorry to see him soon after gardening in the *berceau*. He approached the glass door; I drew near also. We spoke of some flowers growing round it. By-and-by, Monsieur laid down his spade; by-and-by, he recommenced conversation, passed to other subjects, and finally touched a point of interest.

Conscious that his proceeding of that day was specially open to a charge of extravagance, M. Paul half apologized; he half regretted, too, the fitfulness of his moods, yet he hinted that some allowance ought to be made for him. "But," said he, "I can hardly expect it at your hands, Miss Lucy; you know neither me, nor my position, nor my history."

His history. I took up the word at once; I pursued the idea.

"No, Monsieur," I rejoined. "Of course, as you say, I know neither your history, nor your position, nor your sacrifices, nor any of your sorrows, trials, affections, or fidelities. Oh, no! I know nothing about you;

Before the fragile covenant of friendship between M. Paul and myself could be fully ratified, a third power interjected its voice. We were no longer merely two souls drifting together; we were under the watchful, sleepless gaze of Rome. Through the mystic lattice of the confessional—a sliding panel to which M. Emanuel drew nearer month by month—the Church watched her son with jealous intensity.

"Why were you so eager to be friends with M. Paul?" the reader might ask. "Had he not already proven his partiality?"

Yes, he had. Yet, I found a peculiar comfort in hearing him declare it so earnestly. I cherished his modest doubts, his tender deference, that trust which longs to rest and is grateful when taught how. He had called me "sister." It was well. As long as he confided in me, I was willing to bear that title, provided he did not invite me to fill it for some future wife. Given his vow of celibacy, this dilemma seemed safe.

That evening, I spent the long hours pondering our interview, wishing for the dawn to break so I could finally face the reality of our new arrangement. I wanted to test the waters: to observe how much of a brother he would become when we met again, and to gauge how much of a sister I felt myself. Could I summon a sister's courage, and would he offer a brother's frankness?

When he arrived the next day, life proved its cruel construction: the event never matches the expectation. He never accosted me. His lesson was delivered quietly, mildly, and gravely. He was fatherly to his pupils, but he was not brotherly to me. Before leaving the class, I expected a smile, perhaps a word; instead, I received only a hurried, shy nod.

This distance, I argued, was accidental, involuntary. Patience would surely dissolve it. It did not vanish; it persisted for days, then grew. I suppressed my surprise and swallowed the rising tide of other feelings. He had bid me make my own experiments, to tease and try him. A vain injunction! A privilege nominal and unavailable. Some women might dare it, but I was not among the brave. Left alone, I was passive; repulsed, I withdrew. Forgotten, my lips refused to utter a reminder, my eyes dared not dart a glance. It seemed there had been an error in my calculations, and I needed time to find it.

Then came the evening when he was to give me my lesson. Usually, on these occasions, my schoolroom was wherever the pupils and teachers gathered, often in the large second division. On this customary evening, hearing the hour strike, I collected my books and sought the large division.

The classroom was empty, bathed in cool, deep shadow. Through the open double doors, the courtyard (*carré*) was filled with light and pupils. The westering sun blushed vividly over the scene, fusing the hues of the walls and the variegated tints of the dresses into one warm glow. The girls worked or studied in the midst of the circle, while M. Emanuel spoke good-humoredly to a teacher. His dark *paletôt* and jetty hair were tinged with crimson reflections; his Spanish face, when he turned, answered the sun's animated kiss with a smile.

Orange trees and blooming plants basked in the sun's bounty, now asking for water. M. Emanuel had a taste for gardening; tending shrubs soothed his nerves. He revived the orange trees, geraniums, and cactuses with the refreshment they needed. Meanwhile, his lips sustained his precious cigar, that first luxury of his life; its blue wreaths curled prettily among the flowers in the evening light. He spoke no more to the pupils or mistresses, but gave endearing words to a small spaniel—a creature nominally belonging to the house but virtually owned by him, fonder of him than any inmate. She was delicate, silky, and lovable, trotting at his side with expressive, attached eyes. Whenever he dropped his bonnet or handkerchief, she crouched beside it like a miniature lion guarding a kingdom's flag.

As the amateur gardener fetched water from the well, his work spun on. The great clock ticked. Another hour struck. The courtyard lost the illusion of sunset; day was drooping. My lesson must be short. Was it my turn?

Alas, there were more plants to attend to—favorite rose-bushes, choice flowers. Little Sylvie's glad bark and whine followed the receding *paletôt* down the alleys. I put up some books; I should not need them all. I sat and thought, involuntarily deprecating the creeping invasion of twilight.

Sylvie emerged again, heralding the returning *paletôt*. The watering-pot was deposited by the well; its office fulfilled. How glad I was! Monsieur washed his hands in a stone bowl. There was no longer time for a lesson; the prayer-bell would soon ring. Yet we should meet; a chance would be offered to read the riddle of his shyness in his eyes. His ablutions over, he stood, slowly rearranging his cuffs, looking at the horn of a young moon set pale in the opal sky, glimmering faint on the oriel of Jean Baptiste. Sylvie watched the mood, contemplative and still; it irked her. She whined and jumped to break it.

"Petite exigeante," he said. "You must not be forgotten one moment, it seems."

He stopped, lifted her in his arms, and sauntered across the court, within a yard of the line of windows near which I sat. He lingered, fondling the spaniel in his bosom, calling her tender names in a tender voice. On the front-door steps, he turned once more to look at the moon, the grey cathedral, and the fading spires into a blue sea of night-mist. He tasted the sweet breath of dusk, noting the folded bloom of the garden. Suddenly, he looked round; a keen beam from his eye rased the white façade of the classes, sweeping the long line of windows. I think he bowed, but I had no time to return the courtesy. In a moment, he was gone; the moonlit threshold lay pale and shadowless before the closed door.

Gathering my books, I carried the unused heap back to the third class. The prayer-bell rang; I obeyed.

The morrow would not restore him to the Rue Fossette; that day was devoted to his college. I got through my teaching and the intermediate hours, seeing evening approach with its heavy ennui. Whether it was worse to stay with my co-inmates or sit alone, I had not considered. Naturally, I chose the latter. If there was hope for comfort, the heart or head of no human being in this house could yield it. Only under the lid of my desk could it harbor, nestling between book leaves, gilding a pencil-point, or tinging the ink in the glass. With a heavy heart, I opened the lid.

One by one, familiar volumes were taken out and put back, hopeless. They had no charm. Was this something new, a pamphlet in lilac? I had not seen it before; I had rearranged my desk that very afternoon. It must have been introduced within the last hour.

I opened it. What was it?

It was neither tale nor poem, nor essay nor history. It preached and persuaded. It was a theological work preaching Romanism, urging conversion. The voice of that sly little book was honeyed, its accents all unction and balm. Here roared no thunder of Rome, no blast of displeasure. The Protestant was to turn Papist not in fear of hell, but for the comfort, indulgence, and tenderness Holy Church offered. Far be it from her to threaten or coerce; her wish was to guide and win. *She* persecute? Oh dear no!

This meek volume was not for the hardened; it was milk for babes, the mild effluence of a mother's love intended for those whose heads must be reached through their hearts. Its appeal was not to intellect, but to affection and sympathy. St. Vincent de Paul, gathering his orphans, never spoke more sweetly.

One capital inducement was held out: the Catholic who had lost dear friends could enjoy the unspeakable solace of praying them out of purgatory. The writer did not touch on the firmer peace of those whose belief dispenses with purgatory altogether, but I thought of this and preferred the latter doctrine as more consolatory. The little book amused me; it did not painfully displease. It was a canting, sentimental, shallow volume, yet something about it cheered my gloom. I was amused by the gambols of this unlicked wolf-cub muffled in the fleece, mimicking the bleat of a guileless lamb. Portions reminded me of Wesleyan Methodist tracts I had read as a child, flavored with the same seasoning of fanaticism. The author was no bad man, though betraying the trained cunning—the cloven hoof of his system. His judgment, however, wanted surgical props; it was rickety.

I smiled over this dose of maternal tenderness from the ruddy old lady of the Seven Hills, and at my own disinclination to accept these melting favors. Glancing at the title-page, I found the name "Père Silas." On the fly-leaf, in small but clear pencil characters: "From P. C. D. E. to L—y."

When I saw this, I laughed, but not in my former spirit. A mortal bewilderment cleared suddenly from my head. The solution of the Sphinx-riddle was won. The conjunction of Père Silas and Paul Emanuel gave the key to all. The penitent had been with his director; permitted to withhold nothing; suffered to keep no corner of his heart sacred. The whole narrative of our late interview had been drawn from him. He had avowed the covenant of fraternity and spoken of his adopted sister. How could such a covenant be sanctioned by the Church? Fraternal communion with a heretic! I seemed to hear Père Silas annulling the unholy pact, warning his penitent of its perils, commanding the enforcement of a new system whose frost had pierced to the marrow of my bones.

These may not seem pleasant hypotheses, yet by comparison, they were welcome. The vision of a ghostly troubler hovering in the background was nothing compared to the fear of spontaneous change arising in M. Paul himself.

This evening, there was no bright sunset; west and east were one cloud. No summer night-mist softened the distance; a clammy fog from the marshes crept grey round Villette. The watering-pot might rest in its niche; a small rain had been drizzling all afternoon and still fell fast and quietly. This was no weather for rambling in wet alleys. I started to hear Sylvie's sudden bark in the garden—her bark of welcome. Surely she was not accompanied, yet this glad, quick bark was never uttered save in homage to one presence.

Through the glass door and the arching *berceau*, I commanded the deep vista of the *allée défendue*: thither rushed Sylvie, glistening through its gloom like a white guelder-rose. She ran to and fro, whining, springing, harassing little birds. I watched five minutes; no fulfillment followed the omen. I returned to my books. Sylvie's sharp bark suddenly ceased. Again I looked up. She was standing not many yards distant, wagging her white feathery tail as fast as muscle would work, intently watching the operations of a spade plied by an indefatigable hand. There was M. Emanuel, bent over the soil, digging in the wet mould among rain-laden shrubs, working as hard as if his day's pittance were yet to be earned by the literal sweat of his brow.

In this sign, I read a ruffled mood. He would dig thus in frozen snow on the coldest winter day, urged inwardly by painful emotion, whether nervous excitation or sad thoughts of self-reproach. He would dig by the hour, with knit brow and set teeth, nor once lift his head or open his lips.

Sylvie watched till she was tired. Scampering deviously, bounding here and there, snuffing everywhere, she at last discovered me in the class. Instantly she flew barking at the panes, as if to urge me forth to share her pleasure or her master's toil. She had seen me occasionally walking in that alley with M. Paul, and I doubted not she considered it my duty to join him now, wet as it was.

She made such a bustle that M. Paul finally looked up, perceiving why and at whom she barked. He whistled to call her off; she only barked louder. Bent on having the glass door opened, tired with her importunity, he threw down his spade, approached, and pushed the door ajar. Sylvie burst in impetuously, sprang to my lap, and with her paws at my neck and her nose and tongue busily about my face and eyes, flourished her bushy tail over the desk, scattering books and papers far and wide.

M. Emanuel advanced to still the clamor and repair the disarray. Having gathered up the books, he captured Sylvie and stowed her away under his *paletôt*, where she nestled as quiet as a mouse, her head just peeping forth. She was very tiny, with the prettiest innocent face, silkiest long ears, and finest dark eyes in the world. I never saw her, but I thought of Paulina de Bassompierre; forgive the association, reader, it would occur.

M. Paul petted and patted her; the endearments she received were not to be wondered at. While caressing the spaniel, his eye roved over the papers and books just replaced; it settled on the religious tract. His lips moved; he half-checked the impulse to speak. What! Had he promised never to address me more? If so, his better nature pronounced the vow "more honoured in the breach than in the observance," for with a second effort, he spoke.

"You have not yet read the brochure, I

It was a prudent decision for Paulina to withhold further correspondence from Graham until her father gave his blessing. Yet, Dr. Bretton, unable to reside within a league of the Hôtel Crécy, found himself drawn there with increasing frequency. Though they initially agreed to maintain a distance in their public conduct, their hearts drew nearer with every passing day.

In Graham's presence, the noblest aspects of his character awakened and flourished. While his past admiration for Miss Fanshawe had been rooted in intellect, his current devotion transcended mere reason; his entire being, his highest tastes, and his deepest faculties were now ignited by her. Paulina, in turn, did not consciously attempt to guide his conversation toward books or to refine his mind. She simply regarded him as perfect. It was Graham, by chance, who introduced a topic of literature, and in her responsive harmony, he found a resonance that compelled him to speak with a depth and eloquence he had never known. She listened with delight, answering with animation. With each exchange, Graham perceived a music in her voice that refined itself, unlocking a treasure house within his own soul and revealing latent goodness in his heart. They delighted in one another's wit and expression, their thoughts matching with the precision of carefully chosen pearls.

Paulina possessed no natural abundance of mirth; unstimulated, she was thoughtful and pensive. But in her lover's genial presence, she glowed like a soft, glad light. Where once there had been a gentle ice of reserve, now there was only warmth. Graham's generous influence quickly thawed her timid self-imposition.

They spoke of the old Bretton days, moving from hesitant smiles to open candor. Graham, having earned his independence from the reluctant Lucy, recounted his memories of "little Polly" with a kindness that made any suggestion from me seem pale by comparison. Paulina marveled at the richness of his memory. He recalled how she once gathered his head in her arms, crying out her affection, and how she climbed to his knee. He remembered the sensation of her small hands smoothing his cheek, the trembling touch of her finger in his chin's cleft, and the curious lisp with which she called it a "pretty dimple."

"Child as I was," Paulina admitted, her voice trembling slightly, "I wonder how I dared be so venturesome. To me, he seems now all sacred. His locks are inaccessible. When I look at his firm, marble chin, his straight Greek features, I feel a sort of fear. Women are called beautiful, Lucy; he is not like a woman. What is he, then? Do other people see him with my eyes? Do *you* admire him?"

"I'll tell you what I do, Paulina," I replied, silencing her tender confidences before they could melt into molten lead in my ear. "_I never see him_. I looked at him twice or thrice a year ago, before he recognized me, and then I shut my eyes. If he were to cross their balls twelve times between sunset and sunrise, I should hardly know what shape had gone by."

"Lucy, what do you mean?" she whispered.

"I mean that I value vision, and dread being struck stone blind."

Best to answer strongly and forever silence those passionate confidences that left her lips tasting of sweet honey. Yet, she would speak of him anyway—sometimes shyly, sometimes with a tenderness of cadence that chafed me miserably. Cloudless happiness had dazzled her native clear sight, making her view of me fitful.

"Spartan girl! Proud Lucy!" she would smile. "Graham says you are the most peculiar, capricious little woman he knows; but yet you are excellent; we both think so."

"You both think you know not what," I retorted. "Make me as little the subject of your mutual talk as possible. I have my sort of life apart from yours."

"But ours, Lucy, is a beautiful life, or it will be; and you shall share it."

"I shall share no man's or woman's life in this world, as you understand sharing. I think I have one friend of my own, but am not sure; and till I _am_ sure, I live solitary."

"But solitude is sadness."

"Yes; it is sadness. Life, however, has worse than that. Deeper than melancholy lies heart-break."

"Lucy, I wonder if anybody will ever comprehend you altogether."

Lovers possess an infatuation of egotism; they demand a witness to their happiness, regardless of the cost. Paulina had forbidden letters, yet Dr. Bretton wrote; she had resolved against correspondence, yet she answered, merely to chide. She showed me these letters with the wilfulness of a spoiled child and the imperiousness of an heiress, forcing me to read them. Graham's were fine, manly, modest, and gallant. Hers, though seemingly written to hide her feelings and bridle his ardor, glowed with an unconfessed confession that kindled every greeting to adieu.

"I wish papa knew; I _do_ wish papa knew!" she murmured anxiously. "I wish, and yet I fear. I can hardly keep Graham back from telling him. There is nothing I long for more than to have this affair settled, yet I dread the crisis. I know papa will be angry at first; I fear he will dislike me almost. It will seem an untoward business, a shock."

The fact was, her father, long calm and blind on this point, was beginning to be stirred by an importunate light. To her, he said nothing, but when she was not looking, I saw him gaze and meditate on her.

One evening, while Paulina wrote to Graham in her dressing-room, leaving me in the library, M. de Bassompierre entered. He sat near the window, opened a desk, and studied an entry in a memorandum-book.

"Miss Snowe," he said, laying it down, "do you know my little girl's age?"

"About eighteen, is it not, sir?"

"It seems so. This old pocket-book tells me she was born on the 5th of May, eighteen years ago. It is strange; I had lost the reckoning. I thought of her as twelve, fourteen—an indefinite date; but she seemed a child."

"She is about eighteen," I repeated. "She is grown up; she will be no taller."

"My little jewel!" he said, in a tone that penetrated like his daughter's accents.

He sat very thoughtful.

"Sir, don't grieve," I said, sensing his unspoken feelings.

"She is the only pearl I have," he said. "And now others will find out that she is pure and of price: they will covet her."

Graham Bretton had dined with us that day, shining in converse and looks. Under the stimulus of high hope, something had unfolded in his manner that compelled attention. M. de Bassompierre, slow to remark but logical in reasoning, had caught the direction of his homage.

"Where is she?" he asked.

"She is up-stairs."

"What is she doing?"

"She is writing."

"She writes, does she? Does she receive letters?"

"None but such as she can show me. And—sir—she—_they_ have long wanted to consult you."

"Pshaw! They don't think of me—an old father! I am in the way."

"Ah, M. de Bassompierre—not so! But Paulina must speak for herself: and Dr. Bretton, too, must be his own advocate."

"It is a little late. Matters are advanced, it seems."

"Sir, till you approve, nothing is done—only they love each other."

"Only!" he echoed.

Invested by fate as confidante and mediator, I continued: "Hundreds of times has Dr. Bretton been on the point of appealing to you, sir; but, with all his high courage, he fears you mortally."

"He may well—he may well fear me. He has touched the best thing I have. Had he but let her alone, she would have remained a child for years yet. So. Are they engaged?"

"They could not become engaged without your permission."

"It is well for you, Miss Snowe, to talk and think with that propriety which always characterizes you; but this matter is a grief to me; my little girl was all I had: I have no more daughters and no son; Bretton might as well have looked elsewhere; there are scores of rich and pretty women who would not, I daresay, dislike him: he has looks, and conduct, and connection. Would nothing serve him but my Polly?"

"If he had never seen your 'Polly,' others might and would have pleased him—your niece, Miss Fanshawe, for instance."

"Ah! I would have given him Ginevra with all my heart; but Polly!—I can't let him have her. No—I can't. He is not her equal," he affirmed, rather gruffly. "In what particular is he her match? They talk of fortune! I am not an avaricious or interested man, but the world thinks of these things—and Polly will be rich."

"Yes, that is known," said I: "all Villette knows her as an heiress."

"Do they talk of my little girl in that light?"

"They do, sir."

He fell into deep thought. I ventured, "Would you, sir, think anyone Paulina's match? Would you prefer any other to Dr. Bretton? Do you think higher rank or more wealth would make much difference in your feelings towards a future son-in-law?"

"You touch me there," said he.

"Look at the aristocracy of Villette—you would not like them, sir?"

"I should not—never a duc, baron, or vicomte of the lot."

"I am told many of these persons think about her, sir," I went on, gaining courage. "Other suitors will come, therefore, if Dr. Bretton is refused. Independent of heiress-ship, it appears to me that Paulina charms most of those who see her."

"Does she? How? My little girl is not thought a beauty."

"Sir, Miss de Bassompierre is very beautiful."

"Nonsense!—begging your pardon, Miss Snowe, but I think you are too partial. I like Polly: I like all her ways and all her looks—but then I am her father; and even I never thought about beauty. She is amusing, fairy-like, interesting to me;—you must be mistaken in supposing her handsome?"

"She attracts, sir: she would attract without the advantages of your wealth and position."

"My wealth and position! Are these any bait to Graham? If I thought so——"

"Dr. Bretton knows these points perfectly, as you may be sure, M. de Bassompierre, and values them as any gentleman would—as _you_ would yourself, under the same circumstances—but they are not his baits. He loves your daughter very much; he feels her finest qualities, and they influence him worthily."

"What! has my little pet 'fine qualities?'"

"Ah, sir! did you observe her that evening when so many men of eminence and learning dined here?"

"I certainly was rather struck and surprised with her manner that day; its womanliness made me smile."

"And did you see those accomplished Frenchmen gather round her in the drawing-room?"

"I did; but I thought it was by way of relaxation—as one might amuse oneself with a pretty infant."

"Sir, she demeaned herself with distinction; and I heard the French gentlemen say she was 'pétrie d'esprit et de graces.' Dr. Bretton thought the same."

"She is a good, dear child, that is certain; and I _do_ believe she has some character. When I think of it, I was once ill; Polly nursed me; they thought I should die; she, I recollect, grew at once stronger and tenderer as I grew worse in health. And as I recovered, what a sunbeam she was in my sick-room! Yes; she played about my chair as noiselessly and as cheerful as light. And now she is sought in marriage! I don't want to part with her," he said, and he groaned.

"You have known Dr. and Mrs. Bretton so long," I suggested, "it would be less like separation to give her to him than to another."

He reflected rather gloomily.

"True. I have long known Louisa Bretton," he murmured. "She and I are indeed old, old friends; a sweet, kind girl she was when she was young. You talk of beauty, Miss Snowe! _she_ was handsome, if you will—tall, straight, and blooming—not the mere child or elf my Polly seems to me: at eighteen, Louisa had a carriage and stature fit for a princess. She is a comely and a good woman now. The lad is like her; I have always thought so, and favoured and wished him well. Now he repays me by this robbery! My little treasure used to love her old father dearly and truly. It is all over now, doubtless—I am an incumbrance."

The door opened—his "little treasure" came in. She was dressed in evening beauty; that animation which sometimes comes with the close of day warmed her eye and cheek; a tinge of summer crimson heightened her complexion; her curls fell full and long on her lily neck; her white dress suited the heat of June. Thinking me alone, she had brought in her hand the letter just written—brought it folded but unsealed. I was to read it. When she saw her father, her tripping step faltered, paused a moment—the colour in her cheek flowed rosy over her whole face.

"Polly," said M. de Bassompierre, in a low voice, with a grave smile, "do you blush at seeing papa? That is something new."

"I don't blush—I never _do_ blush," she affirmed, while another eddy from the heart sent up its scarlet. "But I thought you were in the dining-room, and I wanted Lucy."

"You thought I was with John Graham Bret

But the decree of fate is absolute; it shall be fulfilled whether we bow in humble resignation or stand defiant. The creative impulse drives it forward; the strength of visible and invisible powers demands its completion. Proof of a life beyond must be forged, perhaps in fire and blood if necessity dictates. Nature itself bears this record written in flame and sacrifice, and so does our own experience. Sufferer, do not faint before this burning evidence. Tired traveler, gird your loins; look upward and march on. Fellow pilgrims and mourners, join in friendly company. The path through this world's wilderness is dark for most of us, yet let our tread be equal and steady; let our cross serve as our banner. Our staff is His promise, whose word is tried and whose way perfect; our present hope is His providence, who grants the shield of salvation and whose gentleness makes great; our final home is His bosom, dwelling in the heights of Heaven; our crowning prize is a glory exceeding and eternal. Let us run so that we may obtain; let us endure hardship as good soldiers; let us finish our course and keep the faith, relying on an issue where we shall come off more than conquerors: "Art thou not from everlasting mine Holy One? WE SHALL NOT DIE!"

On a Thursday morning, the school was assembled in the classroom, awaiting the literature lesson. The hour arrived, and we waited for the master.

The pupils of the first class sat very still. The compositions, cleanly written and prepared since the last lesson, lay ready before them, neatly tied with ribbons, waiting to be gathered by the Professor's hand as he made his rapid rounds. It was July; the morning was fine, and the glass door stood ajar, through which a fresh breeze played. Plants growing at the lintel waved, bent, and looked in, seeming to whisper tidings.

M. Emanuel was not always punctual; we scarcely wondered at his slight delay, but we wondered when the door finally opened. Instead of him, with his swiftness and fire, there came quietly upon us the cautious Madame Beck.

She approached M. Paul's desk, stood before it, and drew the light shawl covering her shoulders closer. Beginning to speak in low yet firm tones, with a fixed gaze, she announced, "This morning there will be no lesson of literature."

After a pause of about two minutes, she continued.

"It is probable the lessons will be suspended for a week. I shall require at least that space of time to find an efficient substitute for M. Emanuel. Meanwhile, it shall be our study to fill the blanks usefully."

"Your Professor, ladies," she went on, "intends, if possible, duly to take leave of you. At the present moment he has not leisure for that ceremony. He is preparing for a long voyage. A very sudden and urgent summons of duty calls him to a great distance. He has decided to leave Europe for an indefinite time. Perhaps he may tell you more himself. Ladies, instead of the usual lesson with M. Emanuel, you will, this morning, read English with Mademoiselle Lucy."

She bowed her head courteously, drew her shawl folds tighter, and passed from the class.

A great silence fell, followed by a murmur around the room. I believe some pupils wept.

Time elapsed. The noise, whispering, and occasional sobbing increased. I became conscious of a relaxation of discipline, a growing disorder, as if my girls felt that vigilance was withdrawn and surveillance had virtually left the class. Habit and a sense of duty enabled me to rally quickly, to rise in my usual way, to speak in my usual tone, to enjoin, and finally to establish quiet. I made the English reading long and close, keeping them at it the whole morning. I remember feeling a sentiment of impatience toward the pupils who sobbed. Indeed, their emotion was of little value; it was merely hysterical agitation. I told them so unsparingly, half ridiculing them. I was severe. The truth was, I could not bear their tears or that gasping sound. A rather weak-minded, low-spirited pupil kept it up when the others had ceased; relentless necessity obliged and assisted me to accost her so that she dared not continue the demonstration, forced to conquer the convulsion.

That girl would have had a right to hate me, except that when school ended and her companions departed, I ordered her to stay. When they were gone, I did what I had never done to one among them before—I pressed her to my heart and kissed her cheek. But this impulse yielded quickly; I speedily put her out of the class, for upon that poignant strain, she wept more bitterly than ever.

I filled every minute of that day with occupation and should have liked to sit up all night if I might have kept a candle burning. The night, however, proved a bad time, leaving bad effects that prepared me ill for the next day's ordeal of insufferable gossip. Of course, this news fell under general discussion. Some little reserve accompanied the first surprise, but that soon wore off. Every mouth opened; every tongue wagged; teachers, pupils, and even the servants mouthed the name of "Emanuel." He, whose connection with the school was contemporary with its commencement, thus suddenly to withdraw! All felt it strange.

They talked so much, so long, so often that, out of the multitude of their words and rumors, some intelligence eventually grew. About the third day, I heard it said that he was to sail in a week; then—that he was bound for the West Indies. I looked at Madame Beck's face and into her eyes for disproof or confirmation of this report; I perused her all over for information, but no part of her disclosed more than what was unperturbed and commonplace.

"This secession was an immense loss to her," she alleged. "She did not know how she should fill up the vacancy. She was so used to her kinsman; he had become her right hand; what should she do without him? She had opposed the step, but M. Paul had convinced her it was his duty."

She said all this in public, in class, at the dinner table, speaking audibly to Zélie St. Pierre.

"Why was it his duty?" I could have asked her. I had impulses to take hold of her suddenly, as she calmly passed me in class, to stretch out my hand and grasp her fast, saying, "Stop. Let us hear the conclusion of the whole matter. *Why* is it his duty to go into banishment?" But Madame always addressed some other teacher and never looked at me, never seeming conscious that I could have a care in the question.

The week wore on. Nothing more was said about M. Emanuel coming to bid us goodbye, and none seemed anxious for his coming; none questioned whether or not he would come; none betrayed torment lest he should depart silent and unseen. Incessantly did they talk, and never, in all their talk, touched on this vital point. As to Madame, she of course could see him and say to him as much as she pleased. What should *she* care whether or not he appeared in the schoolroom?

The week consumed. We were told that he was going on such a day, that his destination was "Basseterre in Guadaloupe"; the business which called him abroad related to a friend's interests, not his own; I thought as much.

"Basseterre in Guadaloupe." I had little sleep about this time, but whenever I *did* slumber, it followed infallibly that I was quickly roused with a start, while the words "Basseterre," "Guadaloupe," seemed pronounced over my pillow or ran athwart the darkness round and before me in zigzag characters of red or violet light.

For what I felt there was no help, and how could I help feeling? M. Emanuel had been very kind to me of late days; he had been growing hourly better and kinder. It was now a month since we had settled the theological difference, and in all that time there had been no quarrel. Nor had our peace been the cold daughter of divorce; we had not lived aloof. He had come oftener, talked with me more than before, spending hours with me with temper soothed, with eye content, with manner home-like and mild. Kind subjects of conversation had grown between us; he had inquired into my plans of life, and I had communicated them. The school project pleased him; he made me repeat it more than once, though he called it an Alnaschar dream. The jar was over; the mutual understanding was settling and fixing; feelings of union and hope made themselves profoundly felt in the heart; affection, deep esteem, and dawning trust had each fastened its bond.

What quiet lessons I had about this time! No more taunts on my "intellect," no more menaces of grating public shows!
